                                                                               in the last 16000

      **Three things were each necessary, and the literature had named two
      of them.** (1) THE TIPS: Field et al.'s disk map puts a finite body of
      this I* (0.17 in the paper's units is ~0.017 in the disk definition) in
      the fluttering/chaotic band; Pesavento & Wang's card is a section of an
      infinite strip. The domain is periodic, so the strip is free: the span
      fills z with a 2-cell pad past the seam, the body is PLANAR by symmetry
      (d3-body.mjs `planar`, d3_physics.wgsl PLANAR), and the sponge is OFF on
      the span axis -- a band there has no far field to relax toward and glued
      the strip at v_x = 0.001 (measured, the first run). (2) THE EDGES: the
      2D card is an ELLIPSE (card-params.mjs); the 3D plate was a sharp
      rectangle. A stadium section (`?edge=1`, r = c) is as close as ROUNDBOX
      gets, and it moved the strip from bounded flutter to a net drift
      through a full turn. (3) RESOLUTION AT THE BODY: at `?levels=2` the body
      sits at 64 fine cells per chord, tau 0.5087, chi band 0.75 L0 cells --
      the 2D page runs 256 per chord at tau 0.535 with a 0.375 band. One more
      rung (128/chord, tau 0.517, band 0.375 -- still 2x coarser than 2D) is
      what turned the intermittent drift into autorotation. This is M8.0's
      "AMR is the stability mechanism" and D4's "the band is a knob" holding
      at the same time.

      **REPRODUCED FROM THE OTHER RELEASE ANGLE.** `tilt=0.15` (the default
      shallow release, 7x smaller than the 1.047 above) on the same L3
      stadium strip flutters for ~18000 steps -- net wandering in 0.02..0.20
      while arc climbs to 1.9 -- and THEN goes over: net 0.37 / 0.79 / 1.17 /
      1.38 / 1.89 / 2.35 at 20000..30000, i.e. ~0.20 rev per 1000 steps
      against the steep release's 0.22. Same attractor, same rate, a 3x
      longer transient. So for the strip the finite plate's finding is
      inverted: FLUTTER IS THE TRANSIENT AND TUMBLING IS THE ATTRACTOR, from
      both release angles. Two runs, two draws, both tumbling.

      **AND IT IS THE TIPS, NOT THE FIELD.** The strip changed four things at
      once -- no tips, a periodic span with no sponge, an 8n-wide box, and
      (only the strip was ever run this way) stadium edges at L3. Separated
      by running the FINITE 2:1 plate with the stadium edges at L3 twice
      (`?wide=`, `?zsponge=`, `?zclear=` on the card):

          finite 2:1, old box 192x160x192, sponge on every face   net max 0.370  arc 5.09  flutter
          finite 2:1, strip's box 192x256x128, z periodic,        net max 0.363  arc 5.08  flutter
                      no z sponge, one chord of z clearance
          strip, same edges, same rung                            net max 4.14   arc 5.90  TUMBLING

      The two finite legs agree to the third digit at every checkpoint
      (0.314/0.313 at 2000, 0.050/0.048 at 4000, arc 5.09/5.08 at 30000):
      width, the z boundary, the z sponge and the clearance change NOTHING,
      which also retires avenue 1 below. The finite plate rocks through a
      revolution of arc every ~6000 steps and never nets a turn; only the
      infinite span goes over. A real card (2:1, or 11:8.5) at THIS I* is
      therefore expected to flutter in this solver as it does in Field et
      al.'s map; cards in air tumble because their I* is ~10x higher (paper
      at rho_s/rho_f ~ 700, t/c ~ 0.003 puts I*_disk near 0.1), so the
      finite-plate regime to run for "a business card" is I* ~ 1-2 in the
      paper's units, not 0.17. That ladder is the next experiment.

      **AND THE FINITE PLATE TUMBLES AT I* = 0.5.** The same 2:1 stadium
      plate at L3 in the old box, with only I* raised (rho_b 2.68 -> 7.88,
      g_eff 1.28e-4 -> 4.36e-5): net 0.34 / 0.79 / 1.18 / 1.64 / 2.09 / 2.51
      / 2.86 / 3.25 / 3.67 at steps 8000..24000, a steady 0.21 rev per 1000
      -- the strip's rate -- with net/arc rising to 0.83. (The run was cut at
      24000 by a memory watchdog; the series is unambiguous.) And at I* = 0.3
      (rho_b 4.73): net 0.41 / 0.71 / 1.05 / 1.48 / 1.93 / 2.34 / 2.66 at
      8000..20000, 0.19 rev per 1000, cut at 20000 the same way. So the
      finite plate's flutter/tumble boundary sits between I* = 0.17 and 0.3
      in the paper's units, ~0.017-0.03 in the disk definition -- the lower
      edge of Field et al.'s chaotic band.

      **THE EDGES ARE A MARGIN, NOT THE MECHANISM.** The same I* = 0.5 plate
      with SHARP edges (`edge=0`) tumbles too: net 0.37 / 0.48 / 0.90 / 1.17
      / 1.49 / 1.89 / 2.21 / 2.50 / 2.91 / 3.23 / 3.49 at 10000..30000, 0.17
      rev per 1000 against the stadium's 0.21, after a more hesitant
      transient (net ~0 at 6000-8000 where the stadium was already at 0.79
      by 10000). So for the finite plate I* is the lever and the rounding
      buys ~20% on the rate and an earlier onset; near the boundary (the
      strip at 0.17, where sharp-L2 fluttered and stadium-L2 drifted through
      a turn) that margin is plausibly decisive, and the stadium stays the
      default for the reference-matching legs as the nod to the paper's
      ellipse. A sharp strip at L3 is the one comparison not run. The lever for a FINITE card is I*, as the
      literature said; the strip is what reproduces the paper's own body.

      **What is NOT established, in order of how much it would cost to
      settle.** Two strip runs from the same seed and build; a second seed
      comes first. The L2 -> L3 step
      changes band, tau margin and resolution together, so which of the three
      mattered is not separated (the band alone is `?chiEps=`, cheap). The
      stadium is not an ellipse; an elliptic cylinder is the honest body and
      is ~an hour in d3-body.mjs. The tumble's period (~4500 steps) and
      descent (mean v_x 0.027, with lateral drift) have not been put beside
      the 2D page's at the same parameters, which is the comparison this
      leg exists for. And I* = 0.17 sits near Andersen et al.'s flutter/
      tumble boundary in the first place, so a short I* ladder (0.1 .. 0.3)
      on the L3 strip would say whether the boundary is where the paper puts
      it, which is a sharper claim than "it tumbles at one point".

      The knobs: `?spanfill=1` (strip; `span` becomes the z extent in chords,
      lateral width 8n = the 2D card's BLOCKAGE 8), `?edge=` (0..1, edge
      radius as a fraction of the semi-thickness). The sponge mask
      (SPONGE_AX/AY/AZ) and the integrator's `planar` are general.

##### AVENUES NOT YET TRIED, and what each would show

Recorded 2026-09-12 so a wider search can start from the list rather than
rebuild it. Every one of these needs something the six swept levers did not:
a scenario change, a longer run, or a different collision operator. They are
ordered by discriminating power per GPU-hour, and the first two share a
cause -- **the domain the card falls through is small, and in ways that were
never chosen.**

1. **THE DOMAIN IS NARROW, AND THE SPAN SWEEP MADE IT NARROWER.** Narrow
   domains damp (2D experience, the user's). `card`'s free-fall dims are
   `[6n, 5n, max(3n, 2*span*n + 2n)]`, and the plate's chord lies along y
   with its span along z, so:

          span   dims           plate      blockage   clearance
            1    192x160x128    32 x 32      5.00%    5.0 chords, 4.0 spans
            2    192x160x192    32 x 64      6.67%    5.0 chords, 3.0 spans
            4    192x160x320    32 x 128     8.00%    5.0 chords, 2.5 spans

   **THE SCENARIO'S OWN HEADER ALREADY FLAGS THIS**: it gives a PRESCRIBED
   leg a wider box (`[8n, 8n, ...]`, 1.56%) precisely because "the free-fall
   domain puts 5.0% of its cross-section behind the plate ... and a bluff
   body at 5% blockage carries a wall correction of order +12%". Every
   configuration in the search above ran at 5.0% or worse.

   **AND IT CONFOUNDS THE ONE RESULT THAT LOOKED POSITIVE.** Going span
   1 -> 2 raised net max AND raised blockage 5.00% -> 6.67%. If narrow
   damps, the span improvement happened DESPITE a tighter box, so the true
   span effect is larger than measured and the two partly cancelled. That
   makes widening the domain worth doing BEFORE any more span.

   Test: a domain-size override on the free-fall dims, so blockage can be
   swept at fixed span. Cost scales with the widening -- 8n x 8n laterally
   at span 2 is 256x256x192 = 12.6e6 cells, about 2x today's.

2. **TIP VORTICES: tumbling is a 2D phenomenon and this plate has ends.**
   Pesavento & Wang is an infinite-span study. A finite plate sheds tip
   vortices, the classic suppressor of autorotation, and even at span 2 the
   aspect ratio is 2. The domain is already periodic on every axis, so a
   plate whose span EXACTLY FILLS z has no tips and is genuinely 2D in that
   direction -- but `card`'s dims put z = 2*span*n + 2n, always 2n wider
   than the plate, so the gap and the tips are built in. Making z =
   2*span*n for a deliberately spanwise-periodic leg is a few lines in
   `d3-scenarios.mjs`, and it is the one test that answers "is it the third
   dimension" rather than bounding it.

   Note 1 and 2 pull in opposite directions on the same axis -- widening z
   adds clearance, filling z removes tips -- so they are two different
   experiments and not one knob.

3. **THE RUN MAY BE SHORT.** 26000 steps is ~8 flutter periods at the
   observed rate. A limit cycle that is really a long transient would look
   exactly like this. Cheap to test (one leg at 3-4x the steps) and it costs
   only time, but the tau wall is the ceiling: Re = 1100 survives 26000 at
   `?levels=2` and has not been taken further.

4. **THE I*-Re PLANE BEYOND THE CORNER SWEPT.** I* went 0.17 -> 0.68 at one
   Re and Re went 500 -> 1100 at one I*. The boundary is a CURVE in that
   plane, so the corner tested is an L and not a region. Lowest priority
   until 1 and 2 are settled, since a wall correction of order +12% on the
   drag moves where that curve sits.

5. **A SHARP BODY AT THE TARGET Re NEEDS TRT.** `?bounceback=1` does not run
   at tau = 0.50436 -- it blew up at step 5000 with `|v|` pinned at the
   body's v_max clamp. That is D4's tau -> 1/2 degradation reaching the point
   of not functioning, so exact-thickness bodies and Re = 1100 are currently
   mutually exclusive. plans/TRT.md is the standing answer; this is the first
   case that needs it for something other than accuracy.

      Two things worth carrying out of it:
      - **Re = 1100 -- the target -- SURVIVES 26000 steps at `?levels=2`**,
        which M8.0's "AMR is the stability mechanism rather than an
        optimization" predicts and nothing here had run. It falls at 0.038
        against Re = 500's 0.031.
      - Terminal velocity is 0.030-0.033 across every Re = 500 row, i.e.
        D4's 0.0316 at the default chi band, reproduced four times.

      **WHAT IT DOES NOT SAY** is why. A 3D plate is not the 2D one: this is
      a SQUARE plate (`span = 1`) where Pesavento & Wang's is a section of an
      infinite one, and the span-2 row moves the number by 0.03. The obvious
      next levers are `i_star` (0.17 here, and the tumbling/fluttering
      boundary in that phase diagram is a curve in `I*`-Re, not a Re
      threshold) and a longer run than the tau wall currently allows. None of
      that is this milestone's business; the milestone's business was to make
      the question answerable in one command, and it is.

- [x] **M6.5a — THE CLIP FOUND A RENDERING BUG THE WHOLE SUITE HAD MISSED, and
      it is the one a windowed run is most exposed to. FIXED 2026-09-12.**
      `shaders/d3_raymarch.wgsl`'s march interval was in BUFFER coordinates
      while the picture is in WINDOW ones.

      The buffer is periodic and the body WRAPS THROUGH IT; the wake does
      not, it trails a fixed distance behind the plate. So the moment the body
      crosses the seam its entire wake is on the far side, and a march bounded
      by [0, N) stops dead there. The picture collapses in ONE frame and grows
      back over the next several as the body falls away from the seam -- a
      sawtooth on the wrap period, which is what it looks like in a clip:
      "a section plane that reveals more and more of the fluid volume".

      MEASURED IN CELLS, not in impressions -- see M6.5b, which is the
      instrument that made it a number. On the falling card at the wrap
      (buffer x = 0.1, step 14600), under an orthographic camera pointed down
      a lattice axis: the buffer box's lit region **ENDS AT BUFFER
      x = -0.3 +- 0.25 cells, and the box face is at -0.5**. The plane IS the
      face, to within one pixel. The window box runs 49.5 cells further and is
      still frame-limited. In frame terms, 8.22% of the picture lit against
      38.45%.

      The fix is one offset, `winOffset` from M8.3, which is exactly zero on
      an axis with no window, so no unwindowed number moves. `?winbox=0`
      restores the old interval.

      **THE SAMPLING WAS NEVER WRONG**, and establishing that is what narrowed
      it: `boxLocal` already wraps, and the L0 volume agrees with the tree
      sampler to **9.5e-4 at every step including across a wrap**. The field
      was always right; only the interval the ray was allowed to walk was
      stated in the wrong frame.

      **IT IS EASY TO MIS-MEASURE AND I MIS-MEASURED IT TWICE**, which is the
      part worth carrying:
      - **An A/B at an arbitrary step reads as nothing.** Away from the seam
        the cut plane is far from the body and falls OUTSIDE THE FRAME -- at
        108 cells behind the plate it is worth 34 pixels in 160,000, which
        reads as "kept because correct, not because it fixed anything". The
        step to test is the one where the body's buffer position is near 0 or
        N; everywhere else the bug is invisible and the fix measures as noise.
      - **An image statistic clipped by the frame edge is CENSORED, not
        constant.** "The lit region's top row stays at 0 while the seam sweeps
        151 cells" looked like proof the seam was innocent. It was proof the
        lit region ran off the top of the frame, which is what row 0 means.
      - A third that cost less but is the same shape: a 1-D line probe through
        a FLUTTERING wake measures whether the wake is on the line, not
        whether it exists.

      GATE: `tools/validate-d3-raymarch.js --cases=window`, stated as an
      INVARIANCE rather than against a reference picture -- where the body
      sits in the buffer is bookkeeping the picture must be blind to, so the
      lit fraction must not JUMP between samples 200 steps apart. Fixed, the
      worst one-sample drop across a full wrap is **1.00x**; with `?winbox=0`
      it is **1.84x**, landing exactly on the sample where buffer x wraps. The
      threshold is 1.25x, set FROM that separation -- the first value chosen
      by taste was 2x, which let the broken build pass.

- [x] **M6.5b — AN ORTHOGRAPHIC CAMERA, BECAUSE PERSPECTIVE IS A BAD
      INSTRUMENT. DONE 2026-09-12.** `?proj=ortho`; `PROJ` in
      `d3_raymarch.wgsl`, mirrored by `cameraRay`'s `ortho` arm in
      `d3-volume.mjs` and unit-tested.

      The user's suggestion, after watching three measurements of M6.5a come
      back wrong, and it is the right one: under a parallel projection with a
      known scale THE IMAGE IS AN AFFINE MAP OF THE LATTICE. `orthoScale`
      turns pixels into L0 cells with one division, a plane in the flow is a
      straight line at a computable row, and a sphere's outline is a circle
      rather than a conic. Every mis-measurement in M6.5a is one this makes
      impossible: there is no foreshortening to reason about, nothing depends
      on how far a feature is from the eye, and "where is that cut" has an
      answer in cells.

      It is the SECOND projection and not a replacement -- perspective still
      reads as a picture, and the two FRAME IDENTICALLY at the target plane
      (the half-height is `dist * tan(fov/2)`), which is what makes switching
      between them a comparison rather than a different experiment.

      Its first use retired M6.5a's own headline number: "8.22% against
      38.45% of the frame" became "the cut is at buffer x = -0.3 +- 0.25 and
      the box face is at -0.5".

## M8 — THE TARGET, RESTATED, AND WHAT IS ACTUALLY MISSING

Added 2026-09-10, after the user read the M6 report and asked whether the
work so far had missed the point. It had.

**THE TARGET IS THE 2D PROJECT'S TARGET, IN 3D: a freely falling body that
tumbles and sheds at Re ~ 1100.** `card-params.mjs` states it exactly --
`BLOCKAGE=8, ASPECT=0.125, I_STAR=0.17, RE=1100, U_T=0.05`, Pesavento &
Wang's Fig. 2 tumbling case -- and at W=512 that is a body 64 L0 cells
across at tau0 = 0.50873. The 2D page has three things for it that the 3D
fork does not: free fall under `G_EFF` WITH the fluid force, a `|omega|`
refinement criterion that follows the shed wake
(`amr_criterion_pool.wgsl`), and a moving window (`off_x`/`off_y`).

**WHAT M0-M6 ACTUALLY BUILT is the substrate, and every gate it passed is
real.** What no gate does is exercise the target: every 3D case is either a
PINNED body in a STEADY wake (Re <= 200, and the sphere cases are
deliberately sub-270 where "there is nothing to time-average") or an
analytic periodic flow at tau 0.6-1.0. **Nothing in the suite contains a
shed vortex, a freely moving body, or Re above 200.** That was invisible
because no test of that shape exists -- not because one failed.

Specifically, verified against the code rather than recalled:

- **No free-fall scenario.** The mechanism is all there -- `d3-body.mjs`'s
  `stepFreeBody` takes `gravity`, `shaders/d3_physics.wgsl` has GX/GY/GZ --
  and **no scenario sets it**. `sphere` is `pinned: true`; `drift` and
  `spin` are both `noFluidForce: true`. The fluid force has never moved a
  body in 3D, which means `spin`'s 6-DOF validation is the integrator with
  the fluid disconnected.
- **No wake-following refinement.** Geometry-forced only: the criterion is
  the body's SDF plus a margin. Sec 1.3's Q-criterion is unstarted. So the
  shed vortices -- the thing being simulated -- are resolved at L0.
- **No moving window.** Listed "out of scope for v1", so a falling body
  crosses the domain and leaves. (BUILT IN M8.3, and for a sharper reason
  than this one -- M8.2b showed no moving-body measurement can converge in a
  finite domain at all.)
- **A card body DOES exist** and this was overstated in the first report:
  `ROUNDBOX` carries three INDEPENDENT half-extents with an exact SDF,
  which is a plate. Only the triaxial ELLIPSOID is absent, and sec 1.3
  gives the reason (true distance needs an iteration; an approximate one is
  the anisotropy trap the 2D project already paid for).

### M8.0 — how close to tau = 1/2 does this solver actually hold together?

**DONE 2026-09-10. `tools/probe-d3-tau.js`, and the answer is better than
the textbook.**

The first report claimed Re = 1100 was out of reach because Config B's
tau0 = 0.50218 is where "BGK cannot run". That was a prediction, and the
2D project's history is the reason not to accept one: it ships at
tau0 = 0.50873 after a great deal of hand-wringing that the fluid then
ignored. So: walk tau down and look at the field.

`debugHotspot()` reports WHERE, not just whether -- the argmax of |u|
annotated with the finest level covering it, its distance to the body
surface, and its distance to the nearest wall. A limit that is really the
bounce-back, or really a coarse/fine seam, is a different finding from the
collision operator giving out in the bulk.

**Bodiless, 20000 steps each:**

    beltrami 48^3   lowest surviving tau 0.50300   (tau-1/2 = 3.0e-3)
    tgv      64^3   lowest surviving tau 0.50218   (tau-1/2 = 2.18e-3)
    duct     32^3   lowest surviving tau 0.50050   (tau-1/2 = 5.0e-4)

**TGV survived 20000 steps at EXACTLY Config B's tau**, with max |u| at
1.1x the velocity scale -- and TGV is the hardest bodiless case here
because it cascades energy to the grid scale on purpose.

**With a body, bounce-back and a wake -- the case the target resembles.**
Sphere at D0 = 16, u0 = 0.05, 20000 steps (62 convective times) each. On
this case the tau knob IS the Reynolds number, so the sweep lands on the
target exactly:

    tau        tau-1/2    Re      outcome        hotspot (cells from surface)
    0.50873    8.73e-3     275    survived        2.3
    0.50600    6.00e-3     400    survived        2.3
    0.50400    4.00e-3     600    survived        2.0
    0.50300    3.00e-3     800    survived        2.0
    0.50218    2.18e-3    1101    SURVIVED        2.0
    0.50150    1.50e-3    1600    NaN @ 4000      1.5   (last finite)
    0.50100    1.00e-3    2400    NaN @ 1000     -1.4   (INSIDE the body)

**THE TARGET REYNOLDS NUMBER RUNS AT CONFIG B'S BODY RESOLUTION.** The first
report's claim that "BGK cannot run there" was a prediction and it was
wrong.

**AND WHEN IT DOES GIVE OUT, IT GIVES OUT AT THE SOLID COUPLING, NOT IN THE
BULK.** The hotspot marches toward the surface as tau falls -- 2.3, 2.0,
1.5 cells -- and in the worst case sits at sdf = -1.4, inside the body.
That is bounce-back and the near-wall layer, not the collision operator in
the free stream, which is a different and far more actionable finding than
"BGK is unstable". `debugHotspot`'s distance-to-surface is what says so; a
finite/non-finite boolean could not have.

**THE COARSE/FINE SEAM DOES NOT LOWER THE LIMIT -- REFINING RAISES IT.**
Same sphere with `?levels=2&refine=body`:

    tau        Re      outcome                 hotspot
    0.50300     800    survived                L1 sdf=1.4
    0.50218    1101    survived                L0 sdf=56.2  <- in the WAKE
    0.50150    1600    SURVIVED (dense NaN'd)  L1 sdf=1.2

The dense run NaN'd at Re = 1600 and the refined run survived it, which
follows directly from the diagnosis above: the instability was at the body,
and the fine level resolves the body. So the worry that the interface would
be the weak link is measured and answered -- it is not.

**AND AT THE TARGET Re THE HOTSPOT LEAVES THE BODY AND APPEARS IN THE WAKE,
56 CELLS DOWNSTREAM, ON L0.** That is M8.4 stated as a measurement rather
than an argument: refining the body moved the stress to the one place
nothing refines. max |u| also rises from 1.4x to 1.6x the freestream going
from dense to refined -- the wake is better fed once the body is resolved,
and still unresolved downstream.

Three more things worth carrying:

- **THE LIMIT TRACKS VELOCITY, NOT FLOW COMPLEXITY.** Beltrami fails
  EARLIER than TGV despite being an exact solution with no vortex
  stretching, because its ABC field peaks at 2.4x u0 against TGV's 1.1x --
  about twice the Mach number. So a tau that survives one case says nothing
  about another at a different u, which is why the tool prints that caveat
  rather than a single number.
- **FAILURE IS LATE.** The beltrami blowups land at steps 5000-10500, so a
  short probe would have called every one of them stable. A marginal tau
  that survives a validation run can still take the target's long fall
  apart.
- **The duct number is weak evidence.** Its settling time goes as a^2/nu,
  so at tau-1/2 = 5e-4 it has not converged in 20000 steps and is reporting
  0.1x its own velocity scale. It survived; it did not finish.

**STABILITY IS NOT ACCURACY, and the probe answers only the first.** The
laminar boundary layer goes as `D/sqrt(Re)`, so at Re = 1100:

    D = 16  (Config B's L0)          delta = 0.48 cells
    D = 32  (L1)                     delta = 0.96 cells
    D = 64  (L2, and the 2D card)    delta = 1.93 cells

A run that survives at D0 = 16 is not a run whose drag means anything: half
a cell of boundary layer is no boundary layer. **So the body MUST sit on the
finest level, which is an ACCURACY argument for AMR entirely separate from
sec 3.5's memory argument, and it is the stronger of the two.** M5.4's hard
requirement -- the body lives entirely on the finest level -- turns out to
be load-bearing for the target and not just for the force kernel.

### The order of what remains, and why this order

Settled 2026-09-10 with M8.0 in hand. The target Re runs, so the remaining
work is setup and resolution, not the collision operator -- **TRT/MRT is NOT
needed for the target as specified**, which is the opposite of what the
first report guessed.

- [x] **M8.1 — ONE UNSTEADY GATE, on a PINNED body. DONE 2026-09-10, and
      the solver sheds at the right frequency.**

      `runSphereShedCase` + `sphere-shed-Re300-D16`, scored against Johnson
      & Patel (JFM 378, 1999): **St = 0.136, Cd = 0.671 at Re = 300**, from
      the DNS that also fixes the regime boundaries this suite has been
      living inside (steady axisymmetric Re <= 210, steady planar-symmetric
      210 < Re <= 270, unsteady periodic Re > 270 -- every pre-existing
      sphere case is in the first of those).

      **MEASURED, three runs:**

          perturb  seed    St       rel err   Cd      plane   anisotropy
          0        --      0.1301   -4.4%     0.7496   45 deg   1.6e5
          0.02     12345   0.1225   -9.9%     0.7487    8 deg   5.3
          0.02     777     0.1305   -4.0%     0.7496   58 deg   19.8

      **Cd is reproducible to 0.1% while St scatters ~6% seed to seed** over
      a 12-period window, so the -9.9% run is noise in the frequency
      estimate rather than a systematic offset -- and a build-vs-build claim
      on St needs a SAME-SEED repeat, exactly as CLAUDE.md already requires
      for AMR Cd. The 20% bound sits comfortably outside that scatter.

      Taking the runs together: **St lands within 4-10% of the benchmark**,
      with Cd at +11.7%. That split is the design working rather than a
      compromise: the frequency is a property of the WAKE and comes out
      within 5%, while the surface integral over a staircased bounce-back
      sphere at D = 16 carries the same ~12% offset the steady cases already
      measure at Re = 20. **So St is the gate and Cd is reported** --
      `cd_tol_rel` is deliberately absent, because gating on the quantity
      the discretization dominates would make this a resolution check
      wearing a physics check's clothes.

      **IT NEEDED ITS OWN RUNNER, not a tolerance change.** `runSphereCase`
      asserts a SETTLED Cd and a cross-flow force under 2% of it, and a
      shedding wake violates both by construction -- so the existing gate
      could not express the claim at all, which is exactly how the target
      stayed out of view.

      **THE WAKE PLANE IS NOT KNOWN IN ADVANCE, and that is the one
      genuinely 3D part of the analysis.** A cylinder sheds in the only
      plane it has; a sphere selects an azimuthal orientation. So neither Cl
      nor Cs alone is the signal and their MAGNITUDE is worse than either --
      a rectified sine at twice the frequency with no zero crossings.
      `projectLateral` finds the plane as the principal axis of the
      lateral-force covariance and hands the resulting SCALAR to
      `cylinder-metrics`' own zero-crossing estimator, which is tested code
      and wanted no second copy. Two structural checks sit beside St because
      a frequency fitted to a non-periodic signal is a number fitted to
      noise: `amp_min` requires the projected lateral to actually oscillate
      (a steady run has zero crossings and would otherwise read as a MISSING
      measurement rather than a failure), and `anisotropy_min` requires the
      wake to be planar enough for a plane to exist.

      **AND THE UNPERTURBED RUN PICKED ITS PLANE AT EXACTLY 45 DEGREES**,
      with an anisotropy of 1.6e5 -- which is the lattice, not the flow.
      With a seed the plane lands at 8 and 58 degrees for the two seeds and
      the anisotropy falls to 5.3 and 19.8: planar-symmetric but wandering,
      which is what a real sphere wake does. The `anisotropy_min` check was
      added on principle and caught this on its first run.
      D3Q19's diagonals are a symmetry axis, and a uniform freestream past a
      centred body is exactly symmetric, so the only thing available to
      select a plane is the discretization. That is the 3D form of a trap
      main-cylinder.js's PERTURB comment already documents in 2D: with no
      seed, onset grows from round-off, which is SMALLER AND SLOWER AT
      HIGHER RESOLUTION, so Cd and shedding strength appear to shrink with
      refinement when the finer runs simply have not saturated.
      `seedMacro3Perturbed` adds the same deterministic transverse
      perturbation (mulberry32, same default seed as the 2D page, so the two
      are seeded comparably). **It defaults to 0 everywhere**, so every
      steady case is bit-identical and no existing gate moves; the shedding
      case opts in at 2% of u0.

      Kept for what comes next: `debugRunAndCollect(n, every)` returns the
      force history in `cylinder-metrics`' own row shape, and
      `debugOccupancy(brick)` reports the per-BRICK occupancy M8.5 needs --
      bricks rather than cells, because a ray skips a whole brick or none of
      it and cell-level occupancy flatters the scheme.

**Superseded by M8.1's result, kept as the reasoning that chose it first:**

- [x] **~~the yardstick the other three are measured against~~.** No gate in
      this suite could express "the wake is unsteady and its frequency is
      right": the sphere cases assert a SETTLED Cd and a lateral force under
      2% of Cd, and a shedding sphere violates both by construction. So the
      cheapest possible step is a pinned sphere in the shedding range --
      Re 300-800, which M8.0 just showed runs -- scored on time-averaged Cd
      and STROUHAL against literature (a sphere sheds at St ~ 0.13-0.2 over
      that range).
      Mostly reuse: `tools/lib/cylinder-metrics.js` already does exactly
      this analysis in 2D -- window sizing from the physical parameters, a
      zero-crossing Strouhal estimator -- and its header says it was
      factored out to be shared rather than copied.
      **Until this exists, "does this solver shed correctly" is
      unanswerable, and M8.2-M8.4 all depend on the answer.**

- [x] **M8.2a — THE SOLID INTERIOR IS UNDAMPED UNDER BOUNCE-BACK, AND BODY
      MOTION EXPORTS IT. Found 2026-09-10, FIXED 2026-09-11.** Found by building the `fall` scenario --
      the first 3D case in which the fluid force moves a body -- and
      watching it destroy itself.**

      **THE MACHINERY ITSELF WORKS.** Step 1 of a free fall gives
      `v_x = 2.729338e-5` against `g_eff = 2.7293277e-5`, exact to six
      digits (at u = 0 there is no drag, so the first step IS gravity). The
      body falls straight -- y holds at 36.000 with `v_y ~ 1e-7` over 800
      steps -- with the right drag sign and growth. Then it blows up at
      step 1000.

      **TWO WRONG DIAGNOSES ARE RECORDED HERE ON PURPOSE**, because each was
      plausible, each was stated before it was checked, and the checks are
      what produced the real answer.

      *Wrong #1: "there is no fresh-node refill, so an uncovered cell
      carries a stale population."* The step kernel does NOT skip solid
      cells -- it steps every cell and only reflects the GATHER where the
      source is solid, structurally identical to the 2D `lbm_step.wgsl`. So
      an uncovered cell has been updated every step; nothing is stale.

      *Wrong #2: "it is the force-feedback loop."* `drift` moves a body at a
      PRESCRIBED velocity with the force measured and never applied, and
      with bounce-back it blows up anyway. The loop is not required.

      **WHAT IS ACTUALLY MEASURED**, `drift` (prescribed motion, no
      feedback), 1200 steps:

          coupling      tau      outcome                      hotspot
          bounce-back   0.8000   survived, max|u| = 0.020     sdf = -1.60
          bounce-back   0.6000   survived, max|u| = 0.119     sdf = -0.72
          bounce-back   0.5144   BLEW UP at step 200          --
          diffuse       0.8000   survived, max|u| = 0.020     sdf =  0.00
          diffuse       0.6000   survived, max|u| = 0.020     sdf =  3.00
          diffuse       0.5144   survived, max|u| = 0.023     sdf =  7.00

      **THE HOTSPOT IS INSIDE THE SOLID UNDER BOUNCE-BACK AND IN THE FLUID
      UNDER DIFFUSE**, and that is the whole finding. `chi` is forced to 0
      when `USE_BOUNCEBACK` is set (common_d3_step.wgsl's own comment says
      the sharp reflection IS the entire boundary condition), so interior
      cells are stepped with reflected gathers and NOTHING DAMPS THEM. At
      tau = 0.6 the largest velocity anywhere in the domain is inside the
      body at six times the body's own speed. The body's velocity is 0.02.

      **A PINNED BODY NEVER NOTICES**, which is why nothing caught it: the
      bounce-back branch reads `f_in[opp[i]*ncells + cell]`, this cell's OWN
      opposite population, never the solid neighbour's -- so the interior is
      completely decoupled from the fluid and whatever accumulates there
      stays there. Motion is what couples it out: a cell that was interior
      becomes exterior, and its neighbours then read it.
      M8.0's tau probe shows the same thing from the other side -- the
      PINNED sphere's hotspot is at sdf = +2.0 while it survives and at
      sdf = -1.4 on the run that blows up. Motion lowers the threshold from
      tau ~ 0.502 to tau ~ 0.6; it does not create the problem.

      **AND 2D DOES NOT SUFFER IT, measured rather than assumed.**
      `index-reentry.html` moves a body with bounce-back at **tau = 0.509**,
      LOWER than the 3D case that fails, and runs clean: bounded forces
      (|f| ~ 3e-2 to 6e-2), finite, stable across 4000 further steps while
      the body travels 74 cells. Note also that the 2D FALLING CARD
      (`main.js`, the fluid-driven one) uses chi and not bounce-back at all,
      and that 2D's only moving bounce-back body is KINEMATIC
      (`shaders/physics.wgsl`'s `KINEMATIC` mode: "prescribed kinematics --
      no force feedback").

      **WHY 2D IS FINE AND 3D IS NOT IS NOT ESTABLISHED.** Candidates, in
      the order worth testing: RESOLUTION (the 2D ellipse is 64x8 cells, the
      3D sphere D = 12-16, so a one-cell advance changes a much larger
      fraction of the 3D body's surface); the interior VOLUME-to-surface
      ratio, which grows with dimension; and the velocity set. Do not guess
      -- the 2D page is right there and takes `?tau=`, so the first
      experiment is to walk 2D's tau down and see whether it has a threshold
      too.

      **FIXED 2026-09-11: `SOLID_EQ`, one branch at the end of each step
      kernel.** Interior cells are written as `feq(1, u_body)` every step.
      Not a correction to the boundary condition -- a statement that the
      interior HAS no boundary condition and may as well hold the state a
      co-moving fluid would have.

      **IT IS ALSO THE REFILL.** A cell entering the fluid arrives at
      equilibrium with the body's local velocity, which is the cheap
      fresh-node scheme applied every step instead of at the transition.
      No extrapolation, no neighbour search, no was-solid-now-fluid
      bookkeeping: doing it unconditionally is cheaper than detecting when
      to do it.

      **AND IT IS PROVABLY FREE ON THE FLUID SIDE.** Nothing reads a solid
      cell: the step's bounce-back branch reads `f_in[opp[i]]` at the FLUID
      cell, and `common_d3_force.wgsl`'s momentum exchange runs only where
      `phi >= 0`. So this cannot move a pinned-body number, and the check
      below is a formality that was run anyway.

      GATE MET, `drift` with bounce-back, `?solideq=` A/B:

          solideq   tau      outcome                     hotspot
          0         0.6000   survived, max|u| = 0.119    sdf = -0.72
          0         0.5144   BLEW UP at step 200         --
          1         0.6000   survived, max|u| = 0.0203   sdf =  0.00
          1         0.5144   survived, max|u| = 0.0215   sdf =  3.05

      max|u| pinned at the body's own speed of 0.02, and the hotspot out of
      the solid -- which is the gate as written, rather than "the run lasts
      longer", which is what viscosity buys.

      **AND THE FREE FALL NOW FALLS.** It accelerates smoothly from rest to
      ~0.044 over 5000 steps, stays finite, and holds its line (y drifts
      36.000 -> 35.9). It then reaches the far sponge at x = 184 of 192 and
      leaves the domain, which is M8.3 and not a defect.

      ONE NUMBER TO EXPLAIN IN M8.2b: terminal velocity lands at ~0.044
      against the 0.040 the parameterization targets, implying
      Cd ~ 0.90 against Schiller-Naumann's 1.10 at Re = 110 -- about -18%,
      where the suite's PINNED sphere at D = 16 measures +12%. Candidates,
      untested: D = 12 is coarser than any validated case, the blockage is
      2.2% against the sphere scenario's 1.23%, and the body is still
      accelerating at step 5000 so it is near terminal rather than at it.
      Do not tune the scenario until that is measured.

- [~] **M8.2b — FREE FALL: the instrument is built, and it cannot finish
      its measurement without M8.3.** 2026-09-11.

      **THE GALILEAN PAIR** is the right instrument and it is now in the
      `fall` scenario as two extra modes, in ONE domain with one blockage
      and one sponge:

          ?tow=U      the body MOVES at U through still fluid, force
                      recorded and never applied -- the moving-body coupling
                      under test
          ?stream=U   the body is PINNED in a freestream U -- the same
                      relative motion in the other frame, and the path every
                      validated sphere case already uses

      They must agree, because they are the same flow seen from two frames.
      That isolates the moving-body coupling from resolution, blockage and
      the drag correlation ALL AT ONCE, none of which a comparison against
      Schiller-Naumann can separate -- which is why the first version of this
      gate, scored against Schiller-Naumann, produced a -18% that meant
      nothing.

      **MEASURED, and the pair disagrees:**

          streamed, converged           Cd = 1.362  (steady from 8 D/U;
                                        1.378 -> 1.362 -> 1.362 over
                                        4 / 15 / 45 D/U, so development
                                        accounts for 1.2% and no more)
          towed, 4 D/U                  Cd = 0.951
          towed, 8 D/U                  Cd = 1.067
          towed, 12 D/U                 contaminated -- the body is at
                                        x = 168 of 192 and feeling the
                                        sponge

      **THE TOWED VALUE IS STILL RISING TOWARD THE STREAMED ONE AND RUNS OUT
      OF DOMAIN BEFORE IT ARRIVES.** A towed body travels U per step, so
      reaching the 30 D/U the validated sphere cases settle for would carry
      it 360 cells through a 192-cell domain. **No moving-body drag
      measurement can converge in a finite domain.** That is a sharper
      justification for M8.3 than "a falling body leaves the domain": it is
      not only the fall that needs the window, it is any attempt to measure
      what the fall is doing.

      Do NOT read the 0.951-vs-1.362 gap as a defect yet. Two points rising
      is not a limit, and the one experiment that would settle it is exactly
      the one the domain forbids. The honest state is: instrument built,
      streamed side converged, towed side unconverged for a reason that is
      understood and scheduled.

      ALSO CONFIRMED HERE: `?solideq=0` on a towed body is NON-FINITE within
      4 D/U, so M8.2a's fix is load-bearing for this measurement and not
      only for the fall.

      One implementation note worth keeping, because it cost a measurement:
      `makeBodyState` stores velocity as `s.v[]` and `packBodyState` reads
      `s.v[0]`. Assigning `body.vx` afterwards writes a field nothing packs,
      and the body silently stays put -- reported as a clean zero drag at
      every tow speed, which is a very plausible-looking wrong answer.

      **RETRACTED IN PART, 2026-09-11 (see D1). THE GAP IS NOT ONLY
      RESOLUTION, AND THE DISCRIMINATOR BELOW DOES NOT DISCRIMINATE.**
      Everything measured in this entry stands -- the ladder is real, the
      D = 32 prediction was met, both frames do extrapolate to 1.22. What is
      wrong is the INFERENCE, and it is wrong at one specific step: the
      argument assumes a missing momentum term "goes as A U^2", with no
      dependence on D, and concludes that a term shrinking down a resolution
      ladder therefore cannot be one.

      The missing term is NOT A U^2. It is A * u_body * dU, where dU is the
      slip between a cell one layer off the surface and the surface itself --
      a BOUNDARY-LAYER quantity, which shrinks as the boundary layer is
      resolved. So it falls down a resolution ladder too, at a rate not
      obviously distinguishable from the staircase's, and both candidates
      produce a gap that closes from both sides. The ladder was the wrong
      experiment, not a wrongly-read one.

      **THE EXPERIMENT THAT DOES DISCRIMINATE HOLDS THE RESOLUTION FIXED AND
      MOVES THE FRAME** -- the Galilean split of D1, which finds Cd falling
      31% LINEARLY in the body's own speed at one grid, one Reynolds number,
      one domain and one blockage. No resolution effect can do that, because
      a resolution effect cannot depend on which inertial frame the same grid
      is described in. Read this entry as the resolution HALF of an effect
      that has two halves.

- [ ] **M8.2c — FREE FALL, GATED. M8.3 LANDED AND THE FIRST HALF IS NOW
      MEASURED: Cd(tow) = 0.963 against Cd(stream) = 1.362 at D = 12, AND THE
      GAP IS RESOLUTION.** That is no longer "two points rising in a domain
      that ran out" -- the tow is flat to about 1% from 8 to 40 D/U with the
      body holding its place in the window while travelling 480 cells. A
      resolution ladder at fixed blockage closes the gap from BOTH SIDES
      (-29.5% / -25.2% / -17.3% / -13.9% at D = 12 / 16 / 24 / 32, tow rising
      and stream falling), at roughly the first-order rate a staircased
      bounce-back sphere converges at -- which rules out a missing momentum
      term in the coupling, since that would hold the ratio fixed. Both
      frames extrapolate to Cd = 1.22, which is also this suite's standing
      pinned-sphere offset over Schiller-Naumann, so the gap goes to ZERO and
      not to a floor. **So M8.2c's gate is a RESOLUTION requirement, not a
      coupling fix**, and the number to write it against is the one both
      frames converge on rather than either frame at D = 12.
      **AND THE FREE FALL INDEPENDENTLY AGREES WITH THE TOW**: terminal
      velocity lands at v/u_t = 1.104, i.e. Cd = 0.894 at Re = 110, against
      the tow's 0.963 at Re = 100 carried to Re = 110 as 0.919 -- 2.8% apart,
      by two different mechanisms (one prescribed and force-free, one
      integrating the measured force).

      **AND THE GAP IS RESOLUTION, NOT THE COUPLING. MEASURED, 2026-09-11.**
      The question was asked directly -- could it just be a spatial
      resolution thing -- and it has a clean discriminator, because the two
      candidates scale differently:

        - a MISSING MOMENTUM TERM in the moving-body coupling (the
          covered/uncovered nodes SOLID_EQ resets without the force kernel
          accounting for them) goes as A U^2, and the drag goes as
          Cd (1/2) U^2 A, so it would hold the RATIO fixed down a resolution
          ladder;
        - the STAIRCASE is not symmetric between the frames. Pinned, the
          discretized surface is one FIXED shape, and this suite already
          measures a pinned sphere at +9..13% over Schiller-Naumann for
          exactly that reason. Towed, the body moves through the grid, the
          staircase phase sweeps continuously, and the time-averaged
          effective surface is SMOOTHED -- which pushes the tow down relative
          to the stream and must shrink with D.

      `fall`'s domain is 16n x 6n x 6n, so blockage is pi/144 = 2.18% at
      every rung: the ladder changes resolution and nothing else.

          D     Cd(tow)   Cd(stream)    gap
          12     0.960      1.362      -29.5%
          16     0.983      1.314      -25.2%
          24     1.062      1.284      -17.3%
          32     1.103      1.281      -13.9%

      **THE GAP CLOSES, AND IT CLOSES FROM BOTH SIDES** -- the tow RISES and
      the stream FALLS toward each other, which the ratio-preserving
      candidate cannot produce. The rate is roughly first order (p = 0.55 /
      0.93 / 0.76 per step, 0.77 over the whole range), which is exactly the
      convergence benchmarks/d3.json already records for a staircased
      bounce-back sphere.

      **THE D = 32 RUNG WAS PREDICTED BEFORE IT WAS RUN, at 13.2% from the
      p = 0.93 fit, and measured 13.9%.** Worth stating in that order: the
      first attempt at it was killed for host memory (512x192x192, ~2.9 GB of
      `f`) with the prediction already recorded, so it is a prediction met
      rather than a curve fitted after the fact.

      **AND BOTH FRAMES EXTRAPOLATE TO THE SAME DRAG, so the gap goes to ZERO
      rather than to a floor.** A first-order Richardson (Cd = C + k/D) on
      the tow is strikingly consistent -- C = 1.220 / 1.226 / 1.223 from the
      16-24, 24-32 and 16-32 pairs, k = -3.8 throughout -- and the stream's
      16-24 pair gives C = 1.224, the same number. The stream's own 24-32
      pair says 1.272, but it rests on an increment of 0.003 against a tail
      rms of 0.000-0.001, i.e. its noise floor: the stream is already so
      close to converged that it cannot be extrapolated from, which is why
      the TOW's fit is what carries this. **Cd = 1.22 against
      Schiller-Naumann's 1.0917 is +11.7%, which is precisely this suite's
      standing staircase-plus-blockage offset for a PINNED sphere (+9..13%,
      and blockage here is 2.18% against the sphere scenario's 1.23%).** The
      two frames converge on each other AND on the number the rest of the
      suite already reports.

      D = 12 sits +0.057 off the tow's own asymptotic fit, which is the same
      observation benchmarks/d3.json's sphere_amr_note already makes about
      the levels 1->2 step: the coarsest rung is not on the trend.

      So the -29% at D = 12 is not a coupling defect, it is D = 12 -- coarser
      than any other case in this suite, all of which are D = 16 or D = 24.
      It also lines up with M8.0 from the other side: delta = D/sqrt(Re) puts
      the boundary layer at 0.48 cells at D = 16 and 1.93 at D = 64, so "the
      body must sit on the finest level" was already an ACCURACY requirement
      and this is the same requirement showing up in the drag. At the
      target's D = 64 the ladder's own order puts the residual gap at ~8%.

      The free fall agreeing with the tow to 2.8% reads differently in this
      light too: it is two MOVING bodies sharing the same staircase bias, not
      two mechanisms confirming a defect. Run the tow to
      convergence in a window that follows it, require Cd(tow) = Cd(stream)
      in the same domain, and then require the free fall's terminal velocity
      to be the speed at which that drag equals `m g_eff`. Every step of
      that is then self-contained -- no literature, no second
      discretization, no second domain. The 3D analog of `card-params.mjs`
      (I*, buoyancy-corrected g) belongs here too, and NOT before: I* only
      selects the tumbling branch, which needs a resolved wake (M8.4) to
      mean anything.

**Superseded by M8.2b/M8.2c, kept as the reasoning that scheduled it:**

- [x] **~~FREE FALL~~.** The scenario, plus the 3D analog of
      `card-params.mjs`: terminal velocity from the gravity/drag balance,
      `I_STAR`, and the buoyancy-corrected `G_EFF`.
      Small, because every part already exists and is gated -- `stepFreeBody`
      takes `gravity`, `d3_physics.wgsl` has GX/GY/GZ, the six-scalar force
      reduction is validated. What is missing is a scenario that connects
      them, and nothing else in 3D ever has.
      Gates that do NOT need a resolved wake, so they can land here rather
      than waiting for M8.4: terminal velocity against the analytic balance,
      a straight-line fall for a symmetric body, and the |L| conservation
      `spin` already asserts.
      Second rather than first because its own gate is a DRAG measurement,
      and M8.1 is what makes drag in an unsteady wake trustworthy.

- [x] **M8.3 — THE MOVING WINDOW. DONE 2026-09-11, and the towed body now
      travels 480 cells through a 192-cell domain without moving a step of
      it.** Promoted by M8.2b from "needed for a long run" to "needed for any
      moving-body measurement at all": a towed body travels U per step, so the
      30 D/U the validated sphere cases settle for carries it 360 cells
      through a 192-cell domain.

      **IT IS NOT 2D's off_x/off_y, AND THAT IS THE DESIGN DECISION.** The 2D
      pages treat every kernel's DISPATCH index as a WINDOW coordinate and
      convert to a buffer index with `(x + off) % W` at every load and store.
      Carried into 3D that is a window conversion in the dense step, the pool
      step, moments, interp, average, explode, coalesce, both force kernels
      and the M6 tree sampler -- which is what the earlier entry here meant by
      "it touches every addressing path".

      The other reading of the same simulation costs almost none of it. The
      buffer is periodic and **THE FLUID NEVER MOVES**; the BODY advances
      through it and wraps, exactly as it physically does through the fluid.
      Then every f_in/f_out index, every tile, every ring and the whole
      coarse/fine interface are untouched, because none of them ever asked
      where the body was. What changes is three things:

        - `bodyDelta3` (`common_d3_geometry.wgsl`) takes the NEAREST PERIODIC
          IMAGE of `p - c`, so a body straddling the buffer seam is one body
          and not two. ONE function, shared by the SDF, the local solid
          velocity and the force kernels' torque arm -- a body wrapped in two
          of the three would present as a torque with no force behind it.
        - `d3_physics.wgsl` wraps the body's position into the buffer, and
          accumulates the true displacement separately for reporting.
        - **THE SPONGE MOVES.** Both step kernels convert a buffer position
          into a WINDOW coordinate before measuring its distance to a window
          face.

      **SO THE WINDOW IS NOT A TRANSLATION OF THE FIELD. IT IS A TRANSLATION
      OF WHICH CELLS THE SPONGE HOLDS AT REST.** The absorbing band travels
      with the body; fluid entering it behind the body has its wake eaten and
      emerges ahead of the body at rest, which is the physical statement "the
      body is falling through undisturbed fluid and leaving a wake that does
      not come back". No frame transformation appears anywhere -- the fluid
      stays in the lab frame and the sponge relaxes toward the lab-frame far
      field -- which is why the collision, the forcing and the interface do
      not have to know the window exists. `d3-window.mjs` and
      `shaders/common_d3_window.wgsl` are the two statements of it and
      `tools/test-d3-window.js` gates them against each other.

      A consequence worth having: the resample box's origin needed NOTHING.
      It is already in buffer coordinates and `sampleTree` already wraps.

      **THE ANCHOR IS THE BODY'S INITIAL CELL, so the offset is exactly 0 at
      step 0** and a windowed run starts bit-identical to an unwindowed one.
      That makes `?window=0` a real control rather than a different
      experiment. Checked properly rather than spot-checked:
      `tools/validate-3d.js` is ALL 37 CASES PASS with every number
      bit-identical to its recorded value -- `sphere-Re20-D16` Cd 2.9436,
      `sphere-Re200-D16` 0.8622, `sphere-amr` 2.9416, `sphere-amr-L3` 2.9015,
      `sphere-shed-Re300` St 0.1225 / Cd 0.7487, `amr-box-RB4` 9.012e-3,
      `amr-all-RB4` 2.293e-3, `spin-N32` |L| drift 0.000e+0, and the three
      diffuse-sphere regression values to 0.00-0.04%. A change that touches
      `get_phi3`, both step kernels, both force kernels, the manager and the
      render bind group has to earn that, not assert it.
      `tools/validate-d3-invariants.js` is all 14 configs PASS as well, which
      is the half of the suite that would notice the manager's criterion
      having changed shape under the wrapped SDF.

      **AND THE FALL SCENARIO'S BODY MOVED, which is half the point.** A
      streamed body sits 2n from the inlet so its wake has the other 14n; a
      body that MOVES leaves its wake behind it, so it now starts 2n from the
      FAR end -- the mirror image. Before the window that mirroring held only
      at step 0, and M8.2b's rising towed Cd was partly the room behind the
      body still growing while the measurement was being taken.

      **MEASURED** (`tools/probe-d3-window.js`, sphere D=12 Re=100 U=0.04,
      40 D/U per leg):

          leg            window   Cd (tail)   travelled   escape
          tow            on         0.963        480        0.000
          stream         off        1.362          0        0.000
          tow-nowindow   off        0.000        480        0.000

      `escape` is the gate and it is EXACTLY ZERO, not small: the offset is an
      integer, so a correct window has nothing to be approximately right
      about. The body's window position stayed inside its anchor cell for
      12000 steps while its buffer position lapped the domain two and a half
      times. `tow-nowindow` is the same tow with `?window=0` and it reports
      the body at x = 648 of 192 with zero drag -- M8.2b's failure reproduced
      in this build rather than recalled from another session. It is NOT a
      numerical reproduction of M8.2b's 0.951 / 1.067 and cannot be: the
      towed body's start moved to the far end in the same change, so with the
      window off it now runs out of domain in under 4 D/U instead of 12. The
      control's job here is to show that it breaks, and it breaks sooner and
      more obviously than it did.

      **THE TOWED Cd IS NOW FLAT, AND IT DISAGREES WITH THE STREAMED ONE BY
      -29%.** 0.985 / 0.975 / 0.964 / 0.956 / 0.952 / 0.953 / 0.955 / 0.965 /
      0.970 at 8..40 D/U -- converged to about 1% and drifting up by 1.5%
      over the last 20, against a streamed 1.362 steady to four digits. That
      is a REAL measurement of the moving-body coupling where M8.2b had two
      rising points and no limit, and explaining it is M8.2c.

      A SAME-BUILD REPEAT of the towed leg gives 0.961 against 0.963, i.e.
      **the towed Cd is reproducible to 0.2%**, so a build-vs-build claim here
      does not need the caution the AMR Cd numbers do. It is still a moving
      body in a wake, so take the repeat rather than assuming.

      **AND THE FREE FALL NOW FALLS FOREVER, WHICH IS THE OTHER HALF OF THE
      POINT.** `?scenario=fall` with no tow and no stream, 40000 steps: it
      accelerates from rest, reaches terminal at about step 12000 and holds
      v/u_t = 1.104 to within 0.5% all the way out, having fallen **1681
      cells through a 192-cell domain** -- nearly nine laps -- finite
      throughout, with its window position pinned between 168.0 and 169.0.
      M8.2a's version of this run reached the far sponge at x = 184 of 192 and
      left.

      **THE FALL AND THE TOW AGREE WITH EACH OTHER AND BOTH DISAGREE WITH THE
      STREAM.** Terminal velocity is the speed at which the drag equals the
      effective weight, and `gEff` was derived from Schiller-Naumann at
      Re = 100, so v/u_t = 1.104 says Cd = 1.0917 / 1.104^2 = **0.894** at the
      Re = 110 it actually falls at. The towed 0.963 at Re = 100, carried to
      Re = 110 on the Schiller-Naumann shape, is **0.919**. Those are two
      INDEPENDENT ways of moving a body -- one prescribed and force-free, one
      integrating the measured force -- agreeing to 2.8%, against a pinned
      body in the same domain at 1.362.

      That is the useful form of the -29%: it is a property of the moving-body
      coupling itself and not of the tow harness, which is exactly what a
      single number from either leg alone could not have said. M8.2c inherits
      a much sharper question than "do the frames agree".

      One thing to watch rather than fix: the falling sphere WANDERS
      laterally, y going 36.0 -> 33.0 -> 41.7 -> 36.4 over the run. That is a
      free body responding to its own wake and it stays well clear of the
      6-cell sponge in a 72-cell transverse domain, so it costs nothing here.
      It is the case that will eventually want `?window=xyz` rather than
      `?window=x`, and the scenario can say so when it does.

      **THE TRAP, AND IT COST A FULL WRONG CONCLUSION.** A moving bounce-back
      body's force is dominated by the staircase: the discrete surface changes
      as the body crosses each cell, so the force oscillates with a period of
      exactly **1/U STEPS** and an amplitude comparable to the drag. The
      obvious sampling rate -- once per cell crossed -- IS that period, so
      every sample lands at the same phase and the "average" is not a time
      average at all.

      It does not look like an error, it looks like physics. Sampled at
      1/U = 25 this run reported Cd rising to 1.24, holding steady from 24 to
      32 D/U and then COLLAPSING to 0.30 by 64 -- smooth, reproducible, and
      with an obvious story attached (the wake wrapping round and catching the
      body). **The flow was steady the whole time**: domain kinetic energy,
      enstrophy, max|u| and the density range constant to four digits from
      24 D/U on. What moved was the sampling PHASE, dragged by the f32 drift
      in the body's sub-cell position -- 0.03 of a cell over 19200 steps,
      which is enough, because the alias has no other timescale in it. The
      SAME recorded samples decimated every 25 walk 1.11 / 0.72 / 0.85 /
      0.79 / 1.24 / 1.54 across the run, and decimated every 7 sit at 0.98 /
      0.96 / 0.95 / 0.95 / 0.97 / 0.97, matching the every-step mean to 0.5%.
      The probe samples every step and `--sample=25` reproduces the trap. The
      streamed control has no such oscillation -- its body does not move --
      which is exactly the asymmetry that would have made the pair look like
      it disagreed for a reason that was not the coupling.

      **THE VIEW'S OFFSET IS CONTINUOUS WHERE THE SOLVER'S IS INTEGER**, and
      that difference is load-bearing rather than fussy. The solver floors
      because the SPONGE BAND has to be cell-aligned -- a band edge sliding
      through a cell would flicker. A viewer inheriting the floor leaves the
      body sawing across one cell as its sub-cell part ramps 0 to 1, at the
      exact frequency of its own cell crossings, on a body that is physically
      standing still in this frame. The slice view therefore reads the body
      buffer DIRECTLY (its own binding, appended after the sampler's shared
      ones the way the resample appends its output texture) rather than being
      handed a host-written offset: a host offset is stale between readbacks
      and the picture slides forward and snaps back every time one lands.
      Both were visible on the page as the whole field "pumping", and both
      are view-only -- the solver derives its own offset in the kernels.

      One thing the window broke and fixed: `readBody` now has more than one
      caller, and ONE staging buffer cannot be mapped twice. The second
      `mapAsync` rejects and surfaces as an unexplained "Uncaught (in
      promise)" out of whichever caller happened to be second. It is
      serialized on a promise chain, which covers every future caller rather
      than the two that exist.

- [~] **M8.4 — WAKE-FOLLOWING REFINEMENT. The criterion exists and is
      MEASURED; it is not yet wired to drive refinement.** 2026-09-11.

      **THE DESIGN INPUT IS THE TARGET, NOT A MEASUREMENT.** Re ~ 1100, a
      narrow shear layer on the body, tight isolated vortices shed into the
      wake; 2D already established a geometry-forced set does not support
      that regime. So the requirement is not "refine more", it is **SUPPORT
      DETACHED VORTICES** -- a set that is not one compact region, that
      appears where the body is not, and that moves with the flow. There is
      nothing to prove about whether a single box would do; the regime
      settles it.

      Q-criterion, per sec 1.3: `|omega|` flags every shear layer including
      the boundary layer everywhere, which is why AGAL offers `S_CRITERION`.
      Needs the full velocity gradient tensor, nine components, not one curl.

      **WHAT EXISTS** (`d3-criterion.mjs`,
      `shaders/common_d3_criterion.wgsl`, `tools/test-d3-criterion.js`):

      - The criterion, with the DECISION under test rather than the
        arithmetic. `make test` asserts **pure shear has Q = 0 exactly at
        every shear rate while |omega| = gamma grows without bound** -- which
        is the whole reason for Q in 3D, and a criterion failing it would
        refine the entire boundary layer while looking like it was finding
        vortices. Plus solid-body rotation, frame invariance, a vortex core
        flagged beside a shear layer of equal peak velocity that is not, and
        second-order stencil convergence.
      - **IT READS THE DENSE L0 `mac`, AND THAT IS FORCED.** A criterion must
        answer "should this block be refined" where the block is NOT YET
        refined, so its field has to exist independently of the answer. L0 is
        the only level with that property, and it is valid even under
        refinement because coalesce republishes each level's moments into its
        parent. A criterion reading a level's own pool could only confirm
        refinement that already exists. Cost stated: localization is one L0
        cell, and below one L0 cell per candidate block it cannot pick
        between siblings.
      - **PER-BLOCK MAX Q, NOT A BOOLEAN** -- the shape 2D's
        `amr_criterion_pool.wgsl` also settled on. Folding the threshold in
        would make every sizing question cost a GPU pass.
      - A **CONVECTION LEAD**, for the same reason the geometry criterion
        carries `MARGIN + manageEvery*|v|`: a detached vortex does not sit
        still while the manager is not looking.

      **EXHAUSTION STAYS A HARD FAILURE, and an earlier note here saying wake
      exhaustion was "expected" and should degrade was WRONG.** The wake is
      BOUNDED -- the sponge destroys it a fixed distance behind the body, and
      the domain bounds it regardless -- so the steady-state set is a
      measurable number, not an inevitability. And a degrade path would be
      worse than the failure: slots come off an `atomicSub` free list, so
      "drop what did not fit" makes wake resolution depend on allocation
      order. Nondeterministic, and quietly worse rather than visibly broken,
      which is precisely what M5.4a refuses -- and it applies MORE to the
      wake than to the body, because an under-resolved vortex does not
      announce itself the way a seam through the body does.

      What that leaves is a real gap: `maxSlotsAt()` derives the budget from
      the INITIAL refined set, which for a field criterion is a domain with
      no wake in it yet. So the criterion is wired READ-ONLY
      (`debugCriterion()`) and the budget gets sized from a measurement.

      **MEASURED, dense sphere Re=300 D=16 perturb=0.02, 48x32x32 = 49152
      candidate blocks:**

          t (D/U)   maxQ/qRef   qthresh 0.1        0.05            0.02
          0            0.093     0 blocks      24543 (49.9%)   49141 (100%)
          20          12.845   379 (0.77%)       422 (0.86%)     540 (1.10%)
          40          13.018   400 (0.81%)       479 (0.97%)     599 (1.22%)
          60          13.115   628 (1.28%)       772 (1.57%)     963 (1.96%)

      **THE SET IS SMALL AND BOUNDED: 0.4-2.3% of the domain**, against the
      geometry-forced body shell's 160 blocks (0.33%). So a wake criterion
      asks for roughly 3-7x the geometry set, not a different order of
      magnitude -- which is the number the slot budget needs and the direct
      answer to whether exhaustion is inevitable. It is not.

      **AND THE DEFAULT THRESHOLD WAS WRONG BY EYE AND IS NOW MEASURED.** The
      t = 0 row is freestream plus the 2% symmetry-breaking seed and nothing
      else, and its max Q is 0.093 qRef -- so 0.05, the value first written
      into d3-criterion.mjs by eye, flags HALF THE DOMAIN AT STEP 0. On a
      dynamic run that is slot exhaustion before the flow has developed, for
      reasons that have nothing to do with vortices. The developed wake peaks
      at 13 qRef, 140x the seeded floor, so the separation is enormous and
      the only mistake available was setting the threshold inside the floor.
      0.1 is the smallest round value that flags ZERO at t = 0 and 379-628 in
      the wake.

      **AND M6.4's BOUNDING BOXES ARE INVALIDATED, MEASURED.** boxRatio
      (bbox volume / flagged blocks) runs **1.2-1.6 early, when the wake is
      still attached and compact, and reaches 4.1-4.8 by t = 40-60 as
      vortices detach and spread** -- crossing M6.4c's ~4x trigger, which is
      where the boxes stop paying and M8.5's macrocell walk becomes the
      answer. The prediction was that a wake-following set is not one compact
      region; the number says it starts compact and stops being so exactly as
      the vortices detach.

      **AND THE BUDGET IS NOW ACCOUNTED FOR DURING A RUN, not only at the
      cliff.** Exhaustion was already a hard failure (M5.4a) and stays one,
      but up to the moment it fires a run one tile from the edge looked
      exactly like a run at half its budget -- and the one number on screen
      was `alloc.activeSlots`, the INITIAL static allocation, which never
      moves on a dynamic run. So the status line now reports LIVE usage and a
      PEAK, `debugPoolUsage()` returns both per level for a harness, and a
      warning fires at `?slotWarn=` (0.85) before anything is refused.

      **THE PEAK IS RECORDED ON THE GPU, not sampled by the host**
      (`freeCount[2]`, an `atomicMin` in `refine`, seeded at the initial free
      count). A host that polls can only report the peaks it happened to look
      at, and "we never saw it above 66%" is a weaker claim than "it never
      went above 66%" -- which is the one a budget needs. The array was
      already `array<atomic<i32>, 4>` with [2] and [3] spare.

      Checked on the `drift` scenario at two headrooms: quiet at 66.7% of
      budget, warning at 95.2%, peak equal to the live count in both. That
      last part is not a null result -- the pass order is
      decide/drain/coarsen/refine, so tiles are freed BEFORE any are taken and
      a translating body has no transient spike to catch. It does mean this
      has not yet been exercised against a GROWING set, which is what the
      criterion will produce and what `drift` cannot: a translating body holds
      `inUse` constant while every tile changes hands.

      **AND IT IS WIRED, AND IT FOLLOWS DETACHED VORTICES.** `?qthresh=`
      turns it on -- its PRESENCE is the switch, so absent it folds out of
      `decide` at pipeline-creation time. **THE UNION IS TAKEN IN `decide`
      AND ONLY THERE**: that pass is already the single producer every other
      one reads, so having the criterion kernel OR into `blockWant` itself
      would make the answer depend on which of two dispatches ran second. The
      geometry half is FIRST in the expression and `||` short-circuits, so a
      block the body needs never reaches the field test at all.

      `?slots=` sets the budget absolutely, because `?slotHeadroom=` scales
      the INITIAL set and a field criterion's set is not predicted by a domain
      with no wake in it. Four refusals came with it, the sharpest being
      `?qthresh=` without `?dynamic=1` -- a field criterion on a set chosen
      once at reset would refine a flow that has not developed.

      **MEASURED, Re=300 D=16 shedding sphere, `?levels=2 refine=body
      dynamic=1 qthresh=0.1 slots=1200`:**

          t (D/U)   inUse   peak    bbox x-span (of 48 blocks)
          0           160    160     9-14     <- geometry shell, no wake yet
          20          399    400     9-18
          40          425    431     9-19
          50          619    621     9-32
          60          644    777     9-45

      The set STARTS at the geometry shell and GROWS as vortices shed, and the
      bounding box extends downstream to block 45 of 48 -- the refined region
      is tracking structures as they convect away from the body, which is the
      design requirement and the thing a box cannot do. Finite throughout,
      `ke` steady at 1.25e-3, `exhausted = 0`.

      **THE GPU WATERMARK EARNED ITS PLACE HERE**: at t = 60 the peak is 777
      against a polled `inUse` of 644. A host-sampled peak would have reported
      644 and been wrong by 20% -- and this is exactly the demonstration
      `drift` structurally could not give, because a translating body holds
      `inUse` constant.

      The budget, sized from the read-only sweep at 1200, peaked at 65% --
      under the 85% warning, with no exhaustion and no degrade path. Which is
      the point: the set is BOUNDED, so a budget sized from a measurement is
      the whole answer.

      boxRatio on that live set at t = 60 is 37x8x10 = 2960 box blocks around
      644 in use, i.e. **4.6** -- above M6.4c's 4x trigger, now measured on a
      real refined set rather than on a hypothetical flag set.

      **AND IT IS A LADDER, NOT A THRESHOLD -- the first version was wrong and
      2D HAD ALREADY MEASURED WHY.** A single yes/no per block refines every
      flagged vortex to the FINEST level, so depth is decided by proximity to
      the body rather than by what the flow is doing. `common_refine.wgsl`
      exists in 2D because that failed there, measured: "at N=3, 118 of 132
      level-2 blocks were inside the geometry halo and the remaining 14 were
      the 2:1 cascade shell -- level 2 never reached the wake at all, however
      energetic the wake was."

      That file is pure scalar arithmetic over four overrides with nothing
      two-dimensional in it, so 3D INCLUDES IT rather than restating it --
      AGAL Algorithm 3, `desired >= k  <=>  epsPhys >= base + inc*(k-1)`.
      `decide` now runs at EVERY level with a `LEVEL_M` override, which
      `balance` was already built for ("this level may also have been wanted
      in its own right ... a vorticity criterion per level would"). Hysteresis
      came with it (`?qhyst=`, one octave): without it a block on a rung
      refines and coarsens on alternate evaluations, re-interpolating its
      region every time, which 2D records as wake lumpiness it was bitten by.

      **THE UNITS ARE THE LEVEL-DEPENDENCE, and they are a trap -- though not
      the trap first written here.** sqrt(Q) is a ROTATION RATE, so
      `epsPhys = 0.5*log2(Q)` puts Q on the same scale the 2D ladder was
      calibrated against in |omega|. The shift is ZERO today because the field
      is read at L0. The day it is evaluated per level:

          Q_physical = Q_lattice * 4^m           (Q carries the gradient squared)
          epsPhys    = 0.5*log2(Q_lattice) + m   (because the 0.5 halves the 2m)

      so the term added to `0.5*log2(Q)` is **+m, exactly as 2D's |omega|**.
      An earlier version of this note said +2m, which is the shift for
      `log2(Q)` and is DOUBLE the right one once the 0.5 is already there --
      i.e. precisely the error the note was warning about, in the note. The
      consequence of getting it wrong in either direction is what
      common_refine.wgsl states: deeper levels mis-weighted by a power of two,
      and "a vortex becomes LESS likely to keep refinement the moment it gets
      refined".

      **MEASURED: THE COST OF NOT GRADING IS ~5x THE DEPTH-3 STORAGE.**
      Re=300 D=16 shed wake, levels=3, qthresh=0.1, demand read off the flow
      with NO allocation (`debugCriterion().demand`):

          qinc   L1 blocks   L2 blocks   L2 tiles   total tiles
          0.5         375         330       2640          3015
          1           375         274       2192          2567
          2           375          91        728          1103
          3           375          76        608           983
          4           375           0          0           375

      **Ungraded, those 375 L1 blocks become 3000 L2 tiles; graded at
      qinc = 3 they become 608.** Two internal checks that the arithmetic is
      what it looks like: L1 is 375 at EVERY spacing, because level 1 is the
      base rung and `inc` only prices levels >= 2; and qinc = 4 gives exactly
      zero, because maxQ/qRef = 11.16 against a 0.1 threshold is a dynamic
      range of 0.5*log2(111.6) = 3.4 octaves and nothing can sit 4 above the
      base. The usable range of `qinc` is bounded by the flow, and the number
      that bounds it is already reported.

      **2D's DEFAULT inc = 1 DOES NOT TRANSFER.** At depth 3 it wants 2192 L2
      tiles, and an allocating run at slots=2400 EXHAUSTED -- 104 refusals at
      inc=1, 888 at inc=0.5, 0 at inc=3. The hard failure fired correctly and
      the M8.4 accounting is what made it legible rather than a stall; those
      two rows are a valid demonstration that the gate works and INVALID as
      measurements, because the runs hit the wall.

      Which is why demand is now read off the FLOW rather than by allocating
      it: an allocating measurement cannot see past the budget, and past the
      budget is exactly where the question lives. Same reason the kernel
      reports Q rather than a boolean, paying off twice.

      **THE KNOWN LIMITATION, stated rather than discovered later:** the
      ladder grades from ONE evaluation at L0 resolution. That is what makes
      it cheap -- every level reads the same buffer with a shift -- but it
      means the criterion sees Q as L0 represents it, which UNDER-READS
      exactly the tight vortices it most needs to catch. 2D evaluates per
      level for this reason. See the metric note below.

      **HOW WE WOULD KNOW IT MISSED ONE, which is the hard question**: under-
      refinement is SELF-CONCEALING. Fail to refine -> numerical dissipation
      kills the vortex -> Q drops -> the criterion correctly reports nothing
      there. It is stable, not transient: once lost it stays lost, and no
      measurement taken inside that run can see it. Every honest detector
      gives the run information it would not otherwise have:

        - **Criterion convergence (the gate).** Sweep the threshold down; if
          St/Cd/enstrophy plateau, the answer is insensitive to making the
          criterion stricter. You do not validate a criterion absolutely.
        - **Parent/child disagreement (the cheap continuous one).** WHERE A
          LEVEL EXISTS the field is in memory at two resolutions. Evaluating
          the criterion on the parent's field and the child's, and counting
          child-says-refine while parent-does-not, is a direct FALSE-NEGATIVE
          RATE measured on the subset where ground truth happens to exist --
          and it measures the L0-resolution limitation above head on.
        - **A resolved reference.** `?refine=all` and compare. Decisive,
          expensive, the shape this project already uses as a control.

      **THE PARENT/CHILD DIAGNOSTIC IS BUILT AND THE LIMITATION IS NOW A
      NUMBER** (`debugParentChildMiss`). Wherever a level exists the field is
      in memory at TWO resolutions: the criterion reads the parent's (L0), so
      evaluating it on the child's as well gives both the answer it got and
      the answer it would have got with twice the resolution. Child-says-
      refine while parent-does-not is a FALSE NEGATIVE the criterion would
      have made had that block not already been refined. Computed on the host
      from raw `mac_pool` -- a GPU pass reading the criterion buffer back
      would be scoring the criterion against its own answer.

      **MEASURED, Re=300 D=16, levels=2, `?qlead=0`:**

          qthresh   field-only blocks   missed    rate    worst gap
          0.5              152            13      8.6%    3.94 oct
          0.3              148             5      3.4%    3.98 oct
          0.15             169             2      1.2%    3.48 oct
          0.1              208             8      3.8%    4.87 oct

      **THE WORST-CASE GAP IS ~4 OCTAVES -- a factor of ~15 in rotation rate
      that the L0 view under-reads on the sharpest block.** That is the price
      of grading from a single L0 evaluation, and it is large enough to say
      that 2D's per-level evaluation would change the answer materially
      rather than marginally.

      **`over` IS ZERO AT EVERY THRESHOLD**: the coarse view never claims a
      vortex the fine view does not confirm. Physically right -- under-
      resolution loses peaks, it does not invent them -- and it means the
      diagnostic is one-sided by construction.

      **AND THE RATE FALLS AS THE THRESHOLD DROPS, WHICH IS THE OPPOSITE OF
      WHAT WAS PREDICTED HERE.** The guess was that asking for weaker
      structures would raise the miss rate. It does the reverse: at a HIGH
      threshold the base sits near the top of the Q distribution, so a fixed
      under-read in octaves pushes many blocks below it, while at a low
      threshold the flagged blocks clear the bar with room. **A stricter
      threshold is MORE vulnerable to the resolution limitation**, so slot
      savings and false negatives trade against each other -- which is not
      obvious and is what should pick `?qthresh=`.

      THREE METHODOLOGICAL ERRORS were found in this diagnostic before the
      numbers meant anything, each caught because a number looked wrong
      rather than because a check failed, and all three are the same shape --
      comparing two things that were not comparable:

        - the child's max shaved a cell off each tile face, biasing it LOW on
          every block (the ring serves the stencil; that is what GHOST=2 is
          for). Presented as the parent over-flagging on 104-152 blocks;
        - the parent's value is a max over the block DILATED by the
          convection lead while the child's is not, so at nonzero lead the
          parent reads a strictly larger region. The function now reports
          `fairComparison`;
        - GEOMETRY-FORCED and FIELD-ONLY blocks were averaged together. The
          body shell is refined whatever Q says, and SOLID_EQ holds the solid
          interior at feq(1, u_body) so its L0 Q is identically ZERO, while
          the child resolves the boundary layer -- scoring as a miss that
          costs nothing. Unsplit the rate read 9.7-16.3%; the geometry count
          is exactly 160 at every threshold, which is the split checking
          itself against a pinned sphere.

      **WHAT IT CANNOT SAY**, and this is the honest limit: it is a rate over
      the blocks where refinement ALREADY EXISTS. It is silent about regions
