      nothing refined, which is where a missed vortex would actually live. It
      answers "is the criterion's view adequate where it can be checked" --
      the near wake, where the tightest vortices are -- and not "did we miss
      one out there". Only a resolved reference can answer that.

      **AND THE GATE IS BLOCKED ON MEASUREMENT POWER, NOT ON THE CRITERION.**
      Measured 2026-09-11, and it invalidates a comparison made earlier in
      the same session.

      St under the criterion, swept over `?qthresh=` (0.5 / 0.3 / 0.15 / 0.1)
      at one seed: **0.0996 / 0.1491 / 0.1269 / 0.1029** -- +-25%, no trend,
      while **Cd is stable to 0.4%** across all four. A flow identical to
      0.4% in drag is not changing its shedding frequency by 50%.

      Then the noise floor for THAT configuration, three seeds at one
      threshold: **0.1269 / 0.1235 / 0.1129, a 12% spread**, all with
      `planar=true` and good anisotropy, so this is the frequency estimate
      itself and not the plane fit. benchmarks/d3.json's ~6% figure is for
      the DENSE case and does not transfer.

      **SO EVERY SINGLE-SAMPLE St COMPARISON IN THIS MILESTONE IS INSIDE THE
      NOISE.** dense 0.1225, `refine=body` 0.1307, the hand-placed wake box
      0.1363 -- one draw each, from a distribution 12% wide. Their intervals
      overlap completely, and the tidy reading "the wake box moves St from
      -9.9% to +0.2%" is not supported by them. It may still be true; it is
      not measured.

      The gate therefore needs a LONGER WINDOW before it needs anything else:
      St's error falls as 1/periods, so separating a ~10% effect from 12%
      noise wants roughly 4x the benchmark's 12 periods. Scoring a
      convergence in `?qthresh=` against the present numbers would be fitting
      a trend to scatter.

      ONE PROCESS NOTE, because it happened twice in an hour: the first two
      seeds came back 2.7% apart and were reported as a tight noise floor;
      the third opened it to 12%. Two points are not a spread -- the same
      lesson M8.2b records about two points not being a limit.

      **STILL TO DO:** a GATE rather than a report -- the shed wake's St under the criterion against M8.1's
      yardstick, scored as a convergence in `?qthresh=` rather than a single
      number. M6.4's boxes to be replaced per M8.5. And the `?dynamic=1` +
      `?refine=wake` refusal M8.4a added can go once the hand box is
      retired.

- [x] **M8.6 — THE CARD SCENARIO, AND THE TWO THINGS IT FOUND. 2026-09-11.**
      `?scenario=card` -- a free ROUNDBOX plate, parameterized the way
      `card-params.mjs` parameterizes the 2D one: chord in cells, `aspect`,
      `span`, `re` on the chord, `i_star` (which fixes rho_b), and an initial
      `tilt`. Built to answer "show me a falling tumbling card" and it does
      not yet tumble; what it did was turn two prose claims into measurements
      and break two things that had never been asked a non-spherical question.

      **IT FALLS, AND THE FREE-FALL BALANCE CLOSES ON A NON-SPHERE FOR THE
      FIRST TIME.** n = 32, span = 1, aspect = 0.125, Re = 200, dense: stable
      to 43000 steps, v_x = 0.043 against a target u_t = 0.05, measured fluid
      force -1.35 against m g_eff = 1.408 -- 4%. max|u| steady at 0.069, rho
      within +-1.3%. Broadside is the STABLE branch at this Re, so it descends
      flat rather than tumbling, which is physics and not a defect.

      **Re = 500 DIVERGES, AND THE FLUID GOES FIRST.** max|u| runs
      0.074 -> 0.16 -> 0.21 -> 0.45 -> NaN over ~1800 steps from step ~23000,
      with rho opening to [0.88, 1.07], while the plate is still within 3
      degrees of broadside. `?omax=0.004` -- a physical cap on the rotation --
      does NOT prevent it, at the same step, which is what rules out the body
      dynamics as the cause. The body forces follow the fluid up: |f_x| goes
      from 1.4 (= the weight) to 200.

      So Re ~ 1100 is out of reach AT THIS RESOLUTION, and M8.0 already says
      why: tau = 0.5 + 6 u_t a / Re, so the margin is bought with RESOLUTION.
      n = 64 at Re = 1100 gives tau = 0.5087, the 2D card's own shipped tau0,
      and `tauAtLevel` doubles the margin per rung below that. **AMR is not an
      optimization for the card; it is the stability mechanism.** (The first
      AMR attempt at Re = 500 diverged EARLIER than dense, at ~6000 steps.
      Unexplained, and the next thing to look at.)

      **FINDING 1: THE GEOMETRY CRITERION MODELLED EVERY BODY AS A SPHERE OF
      RADIUS `shape.a`** (`main-3d.js`'s `geomForced.sdfAt`, fixed here).
      Exact for a sphere and wrong for both other shapes `d3-body.mjs`
      offers. For the plate it was wrong in BOTH directions at once: it
      required a BALL of radius a + margin, about half of which the plate does
      not occupy, while the plate's own corners at sqrt(a^2+b^2+c^2) = 22.7
      lie OUTSIDE that ball and so were not required at all.

      **AND THE GATE WENT GREEN BECAUSE BOTH SIDES SHARED THE MISTAKE.** The
      host BUILDS the initial hierarchy from that same function and
      `debugCheckGeometryCoverage` CHECKS against it, so at step 0 it scored
      the host against itself: 0 violations, on a hierarchy refining a ball
      around a plate. The GPU manager has always used the true `get_phi3`, so
      the FIRST manager pass -- 32 steps in, body moved 0.01 cells -- made the
      two disagree and the gate went red pointing at the manager, which was
      the half that was right. Now it is the body's own rotated SDF, passed
      the ORIENTATION as well as the centre (a plate turns through 90 degrees
      of its own chord standing still). `required` on the card falls from
      28618 to 13850 and the violations to zero; every sphere case is
      bit-identical, since sdfBody reduces to |p| - a there.

      **FINDING 2: THE VIOLATION COUNT SATURATED AT 32** (`d3-amr.mjs`'s
      `checkGeometryCoverage`). The list was capped at 32 entries and the
      caller reported its LENGTH as the count. The tell was a plate whose
      count did not move when `?margin=` went 3 -> 5 -> 7: **a criterion whose
      failure is independent of its own parameter is not measuring what it
      claims to.** The true number was 13454 of 28618 required cells -- 47%,
      reported as 32. A saturating count is worse than no count, because it
      reads as a small bounded defect and `validate-d3-invariants.js` bounds a
      FRACTION computed from it.

      **WHY NEITHER WAS EVER CAUGHT: every body in the suite is a sphere.**
      Not one gate failed; no gate of that shape existed. Same lesson as M8's
      own opening, one level down.

      **STILL MISSING for the target**, and each is now a specific thing
      rather than a worry:
      - **The refine-ahead lead ignores ROTATION.** `common_d3_manage.wgsl`
        says so in as many words -- `MANAGE_EVERY * |v|`, with the note that
        it is exactly zero for a sphere. A plate tip at r = 16 turning at
        the rate a tumble implies sweeps a comparable distance, so this
        becomes real exactly when the card starts to work.
      - **The span.** `span = 1` is a square plate, a different body from
        Pesavento & Wang's quasi-2D strip: its tip vortices are a comparable
        effect, not a correction.
      - **Resolution.** n = 64 minimum, which with the domain this scenario
        sizes is ~31 M L0 cells dense -- so the AMR path has to be the one
        that works, and at Re = 500 it currently is not.

- [x] **M8.7 — CHASING THE CARD'S AMR DIVERGENCE, and the first thing it
      did was retract the claim that started it. 2026-09-11.**

      M8.6 reported "AMR at Re = 500 diverged EARLIER than dense, at ~6000
      steps against ~23000". **THAT COMPARISON WAS INVALID.** It was measured
      with the PRE-FIX `geomForced.sdfAt` -- the one that modelled every body
      as a sphere of radius `shape.a` -- so the AMR leg was refining a BALL
      around the plate, not the plate's shell. Two different refined sets, one
      of them wrong.

      **ON A CONTROLLED A/B, AMR IS BETTER, WHICH IS WHAT THE tau ARGUMENT
      PREDICTS.** Same driver, same parameters, only `?levels=` changed:

          falling sphere D=12 Re=300   dense: DIES at ~8000
                                       AMR:   stable >= 20000, v_t = 0.0230,
                                              |omega| ~ 1e-4
          card Re=500, rotation frozen dense: DIES by 5000
                                       AMR:   stable >= 16000, v_x = 0.041,
                                              f_x oscillating +-2 about the
                                              weight 1.408

      So better body resolution helps, and `tauAtLevel` doubling the margin
      per rung is doing exactly what it should.

      **THE ONE CASE WHERE AMR STILL LOSES IS THE CARD WITH ROTATION FREE**
      (~9000 against dense's ~23000), and `?omax=0` is what fixes it: freeze
      the rotation and the same AMR configuration runs to 16000. So the
      discriminator is a **freely ROTATING body under AMR** -- and that is
      precisely the combination nothing in the suite gates. `drift`
      TRANSLATES with the fluid force OFF; `spin` ROTATES with the fluid force
      OFF; every sphere case and `fall` do not rotate at all. A body that
      turns under fluid torque on a refined grid has never been run before
      this.

      **A TEMPTING CORRELATION THAT IS NOT THE CAUSE, recorded because it cost
      two runs.** Geometry coverage DOES fail for the plate -- transiently, 2
      cells of 13796 at step 4000 and 3 at 5500 -- and step 4000 is EXACTLY
      where `debugHotspot` first shows max|u| jumping with the hot cell at
      level 1 and `sdf` ~ 0, i.e. on the plate's own surface, after which it
      never returns to its pre-4000 baseline. That reads as causal. It is not:
      `?manageMargin=8` widens the MANAGER's reach while leaving the
      CHECKER's requirement at `?margin=3`, which removes every violation --
      and the run still dies at the same step with coverage green at every
      sample including the one before the blowup.

      **THE SAMPLING SLACK IS REAL EVEN SO, AND IT IS A COMPUTABLE CONSTANT.**
      `blockWanted` (and `nearBodyWant`, its host twin) sample a block's 8
      corners plus its centre and compare the minimum against `reach`. An SDF
      is 1-Lipschitz, so that test is only conservative if `reach` is inflated
      by the COVERING RADIUS of those 9 points -- the largest distance from
      any point of the block to its nearest sample. For a cube of edge s that
      is exactly **s*sqrt(5)/4 ~ 0.559 s**, with the deep hole on a FACE at
      (0, s/4, s/2), equidistant from a corner and the centre; verified by
      brute force on a 120^3 grid. At RB = 4, level 1, that is **2.236 L0
      cells of slack**. A sphere never falls into the hole; a thin rotated
      plate does. Not fixed here, because the fix inflates every
      geometry-forced refined set by that slack -- the D=16 sphere's tile
      count goes as (12.24/10)^3 ~ 1.8x -- which moves numbers in
      validate-d3-invariants.js and benchmarks/d3.json and wants to be a
      deliberate re-baseline rather than a side effect.

      **AND A SEPARATE FINDING WORTH ITS OWN LOOK: the DENSE falling sphere
      spins up.** D=12, Re=300, `?levels=1`: |omega| reaches 0.13 by step 8000
      on a SPHERE, which can feel no net torque. m g_eff = 0.0619 and the
      measured f_x reaches 137. The AMR leg of the same case holds |omega| at
      1e-4. Spurious torque on a symmetric MOVING body is a different defect
      from anything above and is the next thing to pull on.

      **REPRODUCIBILITY CAVEAT.** AMR runs are not bit-reproducible (the
      atomicSub free list hands out slots differently), and the card at
      Re = 500 is marginal in every configuration, so the card's divergence
      STEPS are single draws and should not be read to better than a factor
      of about two. The sphere A/B is not marginal -- dies at 8000 against
      stable-at-20000 with |omega| three orders apart -- and does not need
      the same hedge.

- [x] **M8.8 — THE GEOMETRY CRITERION NOW REFINES AHEAD OF A TURNING BODY,
      and the "sphere spin-up" finding is WITHDRAWN. 2026-09-11.**

      **2D ALREADY SOLVED THIS AND 3D HAD NOT.** `amr_manage.wgsl`'s
      `isNearBody` evaluates the card's SDF at the CURRENT pose and at one
      extrapolated `FORCE_REFINE_LOOKAHEAD` macro-steps ahead -- advancing
      `theta` by `omega` as well as the position by `v` -- and takes the min.
      `FORCE_REFINE_LOOKAHEAD` defaults to `REFINE_EVERY`, the exact analogue
      of 3D's `MANAGE_EVERY`. 3D's `blockWanted` inflated the margin by
      `MANAGE_EVERY * |v|` and carried a comment saying rotation was
      deliberately left out because every body using `?refine=body` was a
      sphere. M8.6's card made that false.

      **THE FIX IS THE SAME STATEMENT AS A BOUND RATHER THAN A SAMPLE**, which
      is both cheaper and stronger. A surface point at body-frame radius r
      moves at `|v + omega x r| <= |v| + |omega| * R` with R the body's
      circumradius; phi is the distance to the NEAREST surface point, so
      `|dphi/dt|` is bounded by that same speed and phi can fall by at most
      `MANAGE_EVERY * (|v| + |omega| * R)` over one manager interval. No second
      `get_phi3`, and it holds at EVERY step of the interval rather than at the
      two 2D samples. 2D's own header calls its two-pose form "a deliberately
      cheap approximation, generous margin compensating for the coarse
      sampling", so this is the tighter reading of the same idea.

      `bodyCircumradius` / `bodyCircumradius3` are the two statements of R, and
      `make test` scores each shape's bound against a BRUTE-FORCE ray march
      over its own surface -- must contain it, and must be tight (except the
      rounded box, where inward rounding makes it deliberately loose). A bound
      that is too SMALL silently lets a seam cut the body, which is M5.4a's
      hard failure, so asserting the formula against itself would catch
      nothing.

      **IT COSTS NOTHING WHERE IT DID NOT APPLY.** |omega| ~ 1e-4 on the
      falling sphere at R = 6 makes the term 0.01 cells against a margin of
      2-3, so the existing sphere configs are unmoved.

      ### WITHDRAWN: "spurious torque on a symmetric moving body"

      M8.7 recorded |omega| reaching 0.13 on a DENSE falling sphere as a
      separate defect, on the reasoning that "a sphere can feel no net
      torque". **That reasoning is wrong.** Only the PRESSURE field of a
      smooth sphere is torque-free -- normal forces pass through the centre.
      TANGENTIAL viscous stress does not, so an asymmetric wake torques a
      sphere, and a staircased bounce-back sphere is not smooth either, so its
      discrete normals do not all point at the centre. The literature is
      explicit that a freely falling sphere rotates: Jenny, Dusek & Bouchet
      (JFM 508, 2004) map six regimes for a freely moving sphere including
      oblique and zigzagging paths, and the falling/rising-sphere literature
      ties rotation directly to wake asymmetry.

      **AND THE MEASUREMENT AGREES WITH THE PHYSICS, NOT WITH THE DEFECT.** A
      500-step trace of the same run shows |omega| climbing SMOOTHLY and
      monotonically from 1.5e-6 to 6.9e-4 over 6500 steps as the wake
      develops. At that point U/D = 4.3e-3, so omega / (U/D) = 0.16 -- a
      sensible fraction, not a blowup. Everything then breaks between step
      6500 and 7000 AT ONCE (f_x to 137 against a weight of 0.0619, |omega| to
      1.8e-2, v_x flipping sign). The spin PRECEDES the blowup as physics and
      is then swept up in it.

      **WHAT IS ACTUALLY WRONG THERE IS THAT THE FALL NEVER REACHES TERMINAL.**
      v_x climbs 0.0069 -> 0.0520 over 6500 steps and is still rising when the
      run breaks, against an expected terminal of ~0.0395 (Cd = 0.7 at
      Re = 300 against m g_eff = 0.0619) -- already 30% over. The AMR leg of
      the same case settles at 0.0230 and holds it for 72000 steps. So the
      DENSE moving-body force is under-measured badly enough that the free
      fall runs away, and the eventual blowup is a Mach failure at the end of
      that runaway. That is the defect worth chasing, and it is a different
      one from the torque M8.7 named.

      **AND IT NOW HAS A GATE (M8.8a).** `?scenario=spin&fluid=1` couples the
      turning plate to the flow -- `spin` is the only scenario whose body
      ROTATES, and it had `noFluidForce` hardcoded -- and
      `validate-d3-invariants.js`'s `spin-amr` runs it on a refined grid. Two
      things had to be tuned before it was a gate rather than a green tick,
      and both are the same lesson from opposite ends:

      - **TOO FAST AND THE START IS THE EXPERIMENT.** `spin`'s default omega
        puts the plate's tip at Mach 0.23, impulsively started in QUIESCENT
        fluid; the field blows up inside 250 steps and nothing about
        refinement is tested. `?u0=0.003` puts the tip at Mach 0.07.
      - **TOO LIGHT AND IT STOPS ROTATING.** `makeBodyState` defaults to
        `density: 1`, the fluid's own, which is irrelevant uncoupled and the
        WORST case coupled: the plate shed 140x of its spin in 250 steps and
        then sat still, turning 0.38 rad total. A gate that stops rotating
        stops testing rotation. `?rho_b=50` makes it coast -- 2.7 rad over
        2000 steps with a smooth physical spin-down, 3.0e-3 -> 5.8e-4.

      **MUTATION-CHECKED, and the numbers are the point.** Delete the
      `|omega| * bodyCircumradius3` term and `spin-amr` fails with 164
      coverage violations at step 250, then 61, 32, 3, 6 -- DECAYING in step
      with omega itself as the fluid spins the plate down. That decay is the
      rotation signature; a constant count would have meant something else.
      Restore the term and all nine checkpoints are clean. The body turns IN
      PLACE and never translates, so a failure here can only be the rotation.

      Sized so the rotation lead CLEARS the 9-point sampling slack below: at
      `?manageEvery=128` the plate turns 22 degrees between manager passes and
      its surface sweeps 5.2 cells early in the run, against `?margin=2` and
      the slack's 2.24. Under that, the slack would have covered the deficit
      and the mutation would have passed.

      All 15 configs PASS and the 14 pre-existing ones are byte-identical.

      Process note, because it is the second retraction in two milestones: the
      first was a comparison across two different refined sets, this one was an
      argument from symmetry that did not survive being checked against the
      literature. Both were stated with more confidence than the evidence
      carried.

- [x] **M8.9 — THE BLOCK TEST WAS NOT CONSERVATIVE, and the fix is CHEAPER
      than what it replaced. 2026-09-11.**

      `blockWanted` / `nearBodyWant` asked "does any part of this block come
      within `reach` of the body?" and answered it with the minimum of the SDF
      over 8 corners plus the centre. **The minimum over 9 POINTS is not the
      minimum over the SOLID BLOCK.** The gap is the COVERING RADIUS of that
      sample set -- exactly `s*sqrt(5)/4` for a cube of edge s, deep hole on a
      FACE at `(0, s/4, s/2)`, equidistant from a corner and the centre;
      brute-forced on a 120^3 grid. At RB = 4 that is **2.24 L0 cells** by which
      the test could declare a block clear while it held cells inside `reach`.

      Measured against the TRUE set (dense sampling) over many poses: the old
      test MISSES 10-33 blocks per pose on EVERY box geometry tried, including
      a fat 16^3 cube. Sharp corners defeat it, not thinness alone. Smooth
      spheres escape it, which is why it survived until M8.6's card.

      **LIPSCHITZ BRANCH AND BOUND INSTEAD.** A signed distance is 1-Lipschitz,
      so ONE evaluation at a box centre brackets the minimum over the whole
      box: `phi(c) - R <= min <= phi(c)`. Reject when `phi(c) - R > reach`,
      accept when `phi(c) <= reach`, subdivide only the ambiguous shell.
      Conservative at EVERY depth -- it can over-refine, never miss.

          depth 0   +9..20% extra blocks   0 missed   1.00 SDF evals/block
          depth 1   +4.7..8.7%             0 missed   1.2-1.4
          depth 2   +2.4..4.1%             0 missed   1.4-1.9
          depth 3   +1.3..2.3%             0 missed   1.7-2.4
          old 9-pt  +0.0%                  10-33 MISSED   9.00

      **THE OLD TEST WASTED NOTHING -- it was tight, it was just unsafe** -- so
      any conservative test costs tiles and the only question is how many.
      Depth 3 is **5.4x cheaper in SDF evaluations than the test it replaces**
      and 5x tighter than depth 0. Depth is bought with THROUGHPUT, not
      accuracy: a level-1 tile is ~152 KB and is STEPPED TWICE PER MACRO STEP
      every step, while this runs once per block per manager pass. Trading 0.7
      more evaluations for 13% fewer tiles is not close, and slot exhaustion is
      a HARD failure (M5.4a) rather than a degradation.

      `sqrt(3)` is ROUNDED UP and the SAME LITERAL on both sides: it bounds how
      far phi can fall inside the box, so rounding DOWN would make the bound
      false by a hair and undo the whole point, and a shared literal keeps the
      host and the kernel from disagreeing about a borderline block (which
      `debugRunBalance` compares as exact set equality). No recursion in WGSL:
      three bounded loops with the reject test as the `continue`.

      **MEASURED COST, and it lands where predicted.** All 15 invariant configs
      PASS. Exactly two rows move: `spin-amr` 194 -> 198 tiles (+2.1%, the
      rotated plate, inside the 1.3-2.3% band) and `body-refine`'s tree
      histogram, which is the slot-EXHAUSTION config whose slot assignment
      varies run to run anyway. **Every sphere config is byte-identical**
      (`body` 160/160, `body3` 208/448 both), so `benchmarks/d3.json` needs no
      re-baseline: identical refined sets, identical physics.

      **A TEST WHOSE PREMISE THIS REMOVED.** `tools/test-d3-amr.js` carried
      `assert.strictEqual(activeSlots, 0, 'the premise of this test is that
      the block test misses it')` -- the bug, written down as an expectation,
      years of runs agreeing with it. Its sign is flipped now, and a second
      test asserts conservatism directly over 12 random poses of a thin rotated
      plate scored against the cell-granular checker. Both mutation-checked:
      restore the 9-point predicate and they fail ("pose 1: the block test left
      13 cells uncovered").

      **PATTERN WORTH NAMING, because this is the third correctness fix in one
      day in this one criterion** -- the sphere-shaped `sdfAt` (M8.6), the
      saturating violation count (M8.6), and now the block test. They are
      independent bugs with one cause between them: every body in the suite was
      a SMOOTH SPHERE, and a smooth sphere exercises none of them. The card was
      the first non-sphere and it found all three inside an hour.

### OPEN DEFECTS, with reproductions (2026-09-11)

Two things this day's work chased and did NOT close. Both are written with the
URL that shows them, because every claim below took a run to establish and the
next person should not have to reconstruct the configuration.

The dev server is `python3 https.py` on `:4444` (self-signed; a normal Chrome
wants one click through the interstitial). Both pairs make their point inside
about 30 seconds.

- [x] **D1 — CLOSED ON ITS OWN REPRODUCTION, 2026-09-11: THE MOMENTUM-EXCHANGE
      FORCE MISSED THE FLUID THE MOVING BODY SWEEPS UP.** A free fall now
      reaches and holds a terminal velocity where it used to run away and die.
      **The residual it left is D3, and D3 turned out to be a tau limit rather
      than a second bug** -- no moving-body drag number below tau ~ 0.55 is
      trustworthy on either the dense path or the pool.

      **THE SYMPTOM, reproduced exactly as recorded.** D = 12, Re = 300,
      dense: `v_x` climbed 0.0069 -> 0.0520 over 6500 steps, still rising,
      against an expected terminal of ~0.0395, and everything broke between
      6500 and 7000 at once. The instantaneous drag coefficient, computed from
      the body's own speed, fell MONOTONICALLY as the body sped up -- 0.918 at
      Re = 170 (Schiller-Naumann 0.862, i.e. +6.5%) to 0.262 at Re = 385
      (S-N 0.620, -58%). That shape is the whole clue: the defect grows with
      the BODY's speed, and the runaway is its consequence, not a separate
      event.

      **THE INSTRUMENT: THE GALILEAN SPLIT** (`tools/probe-d3-galilean.js`,
      and `fall`/`card` both carry it now). `tow` and `stream` were two
      SETTINGS; they are now two independent signed speeds, and only their
      DIFFERENCE is physical, because a rigid translation of body and fluid
      together is a change of inertial frame. So hold the difference at U and
      slide the split: `tow` = a, `stream` = a - U for a in [0, 1] x U. a = 0
      is the PINNED leg every validated sphere case in this suite already
      uses; a = 1 is the pure tow. Every leg is the same physical flow, in the
      same domain, at the same blockage, on the same grid, at the same
      Reynolds number. Measured, D = 12, Re = 100, tau = 0.509:

          a        0       0.25      0.5      0.75       1
          Cd     1.3951   1.2697   1.1571   1.0469   0.9637
          vs a=0    --     -9.0%   -17.1%   -25.0%   -30.9%

      **NEAR-LINEAR IN THE BODY'S OWN SPEED, AND THIS RETIRES M8.2c's
      READING.** No resolution effect can depend on which inertial frame the
      same grid is described in. M8.2c's resolution ladder was not misread --
      it was the wrong experiment: it assumed a missing momentum term "goes as
      A U^2" with no D dependence, so that a gap shrinking with resolution
      ruled one out. See the retraction added at M8.2c.

      **THE MECHANISM.** A moving body's discrete surface is a STAIRCASE THAT
      CHANGES. Each step, cells on the leading face cross from fluid to solid
      and `SOLID_EQ` overwrites them, destroying whatever fluid momentum they
      held; cells on the trailing face cross back and JOIN the fluid carrying
      the `feq(1, u_body)` last written into them. NEITHER CROSSES A LINK, so
      neither appears in a momentum exchange summed over bounce-back links --
      and the fluid's momentum changes anyway. Momentum the fluid gained that
      the body was never charged for is drag the body never felt.

      **IT IS FIRST ORDER IN THE DRAG, not a correction to it**, which is why
      a body-scale effect hid in a surface integral for this long. The swept
      rate is A * u_body cells per step and each carries a momentum mismatch
      of order the near-surface slip dU, so the missing force goes as
      A * u_body * dU against a drag of (1/2) Cd A U^2. At D = 12, Re = 100
      the ratio is ~0.3. Fitted from the sweep, dU = 0.215 U -- a plausible
      one-cell slip, not a free parameter chosen to fit.

      **THE FIX: THE SWEPT-CELL TERM** (`SWEPT_FORCE`, both force kernels,
      `?swept=0` to A/B). Each cell whose solid/fluid status changes on the
      next step contributes its OWN momentum to the body, with the sign of
      the crossing. `get_phi3Ahead` is the probe: the same SDF at the pose one
      step on, predicted from the current v and omega. FORWARD and not
      backward because the macro-step order is force -> physics -> step, so
      the force kernel sees `f_in` still holding real fluid in the cells the
      next step is about to bury.

      **IDENTICALLY ZERO ON A PINNED BODY**, so it is provably free on every
      gate in this suite -- with v = omega = 0 the pose one step ahead IS the
      pose now and every cell tests equal. Same argument SOLID_EQ shipped on,
      and the same reason it cost nothing.

      **WHAT CLOSED.**
      - The free fall, D1's own URL: it REACHES A TERMINAL VELOCITY AND HOLDS
        IT. `v_x` settles at 0.0415 +- 0.0002 from about step 9000 to 14000
        with `|f_x|/mg` between 0.95 and 1.03 -- i.e. the free-fall balance
        closes -- where the old build was still accelerating through 0.0520 at
        6500 and dead by 6750. Expected terminal from Schiller-Naumann is
        0.0395; +5% on a body this suite measures +10..26% of Cd offset on is
        the right order and the right sign.

        **AND A NEW OBSERVATION PAST ~16000 STEPS, which is NOT the old
        failure and should not be filed as one.** The sphere spins up --
        |omega| climbs monotonically 7e-5 (step 9000) -> 2.2e-3 (26000), i.e.
        omega R / U reaching 0.55 -- and `v_x` falls back to 0.024 with
        `Cd_inst` rising to 2.4. The old defect was a body that accelerated
        past terminal and then failed in Mach; this is a body that held
        terminal and is now being SPUN. M8.8 withdrew the sphere's spin as a
        defect on the grounds that it grew smoothly to 6.9e-4 over 6500 steps
        and that a real wake does torque a sphere. It is an order of magnitude
        larger here over four times the run, and at tau = 0.5048 this case has
        almost no stability margin, so it is worth its own look before being
        either accepted as physics or blamed on tau. Do not re-run D1's URL
        past 15000 steps and read the tail as a regression.
      - The momentum budget, in a periodic sponge-free box where the fluid's
        momentum can only change through the body (`?scenario=drift`,
        `--budget`): residual 2.8% -> 1.5% of the impulse.
      - The Galilean split: -30.9% -> -18.5%.

      **WHAT DID NOT, AND WHAT IT TURNED OUT TO BE.** The split still falls
      18.5% across the frame at tau = 0.509. **That residual is NOT the
      swept-cell accounting and NOT the fresh-node refill; it is the
      bounce-back coupling degrading as tau -> 1/2, and D3 is the same thing
      seen on the pool.** Read D3 for the measurement: the momentum budget is
      short by 16.6% / 3.6% / 1.5% / 0.0% at tau = 0.509 / 0.55 / 0.6 / 0.8
      with `deficit * (tau - 1/2)` constant, and at tau = 0.6 the Galilean
      split is FLAT on the dense path and on the pool and the two agree with
      each other.

      **A NOTE ON THE FIRST ANSWER GIVEN HERE, because it was wrong in an
      instructive way.** This entry originally reasoned: the budget closes to
      1.5% while the split still falls 18.5%, so the force must be complete
      and the FLOW must differ between frames -- the refill. Both halves of
      that were mistaken. The budget was measured at tau = 0.6, where there is
      almost no defect to see, so "it closes" was a statement about the wrong
      configuration; and enstrophy, which is exactly frame-invariant, matches
      to 0.3% between the frames at tau = 0.509, so the flow does NOT differ
      and the refill was never implicated. **Measure the budget at the
      configuration the symptom lives in, not at a convenient one.**

      **AND THE CAVEAT ON THE SIGNAL.** A moving body's force RMS is
      comparable to its mean (3.36 against 1.14 at a = 1) -- that is the
      staircase, not noise in the measurement, and every number here is a
      mean over EVERY step for that reason. `tools/probe-d3-window.js`'s
      header records the wrong conclusion that sampling once per cell
      crossing produced.

      **RULED OUT AND STILL RULED OUT: the sphere's spin** (M8.8).

      **AND A SECOND BODY, WHICH IS WHAT MADE THE PICTURE LEGIBLE: THE BLUNT
      PLATE** (`?scenario=card` with `tow`/`stream`, `runPlateCase`,
      `plate_cases` in benchmarks/d3.json). This file's own process note says
      adding a shape the suite has never seen is a higher-yield move than any
      individual fix, and here it paid twice.

      **IT IS THE FIRST BODY HERE WITH NO STAIRCASE ERROR.** A ROUNDBOX with
      r = 0 whose body frame is a PERMUTATION of the world axes lies exactly
      on cell faces at tilt = 0 -- the discrete body IS the nominal body, so
      the +7..13% every sphere case carries against Schiller-Naumann is simply
      absent. Measured at Re = 200, blockage 1.56%:

          n = 16   Cd = 1.2081 +- 0.008     +2.4% against the square plate's 1.18
          n = 24   Cd = 1.2031 +- 0.011     +2.0%

      **0.4% BETWEEN RESOLUTIONS**, where the sphere moves several percent per
      rung. That flatness is the claim, not the offset: there is no geometric
      error left to converge away.

      **BUT ONLY IF IT IS PLACED RIGHT, and that cost a measurement.** A cell
      is solid when its CENTRE has phi < 0, so an integer half-extent needs
      the body centred on a cell CORNER and a half-integer one needs a cell
      CENTRE. Centred naively at n = 16 the discrete plate was 15 x 15 x 1
      rather than 16 x 16 x 2 -- a 12% frontal-area error -- and the pinned Cd
      read 1.0024, i.e. -15% against literature, looking exactly like physics.
      Snapped, the same case reads 1.2081. `make test` asserts this by
      COUNTING SOLID CELLS on both parities and both mutation-fail.

      **AND ITS GALILEAN SPLIT IS 3x SMALLER THAN THE SPHERE'S** -- -3.2% at
      the half split, against the sphere's -9.7% at the same split. That is the mechanism showing up where it
      should: the missing term is proportional to the near-surface SLIP, and a
      plate normal to the flow sweeps its cells through a STAGNATION region
      where there is almost none. A body-shape dependence of that sign is
      evidence for the diagnosis; a frame dependence that did not care about
      the shape would have been evidence against it.

      **THE GATE FIRES.** The same half-tow case with `?swept=0` reads -8.33%
      against its 6% bound and FAILS, while the pinned leg is BIT-IDENTICAL
      across that switch -- which is the swept term's own claim about a body
      that does not move, measured rather than argued.

      **AND ITS FIRST VERSION WAS VACUOUS, which is the lesson worth keeping.**
      `validate-3d.js`'s `checksOf()` had no `plate` branch, so it returned
      `[]`, and `[].every()` is TRUE: all three cases printed PASS while
      nothing was scored -- including the `?swept=0` run at -8.3%. It was
      caught only because the fire check was run and its answer was
      impossible. `checksOf()` now returns an explicit FAILING check for any
      scenario it does not recognize, so a group wired in without one says so
      instead of going green.

      **THE FULL TOW OF A PLATE IS OPEN.** At n = 16, tau = 0.512, a = 1 went
      NON-FINITE. A plate one to two cells thick translating along its own
      NORMAL flips its entire face on and off at once -- the harshest form of
      the moving staircase anywhere in this suite -- so it is deliberately not
      a case in benchmarks/d3.json. Do not add it by widening a tolerance.

- [ ] **D3 — RESOLVED INTO D1: THE MOVING-BODY BOUNCE-BACK COUPLING DEGRADES
      AS tau -> 1/2, AND IT IS NOT A POOL DEFECT.** Opened and closed
      2026-09-11. What looked like a separate AMR bug is the same defect as
      D1's residual, seen on a different discretization.

      **WHAT IT LOOKED LIKE.** The Galilean split at `?levels=2`, D = 12,
      Re = 100, tau = 0.509 reads Cd 1.3027 pinned against 1.9203 towed --
      +47% -- while the dense path on the same body reads -18.5%. Opposite
      signs, so it could not be a worse version of D1, and the pinned leg was
      healthy (-6.6% against dense pinned, right for a body at 24 cells
      instead of 12).

      **SEVEN CANDIDATES RULED OUT, each measured at a = 0.25 against the
      +34% baseline:** the manager (a STATIC tile set with no manager at all
      reads +40.2%); its cadence (`?manageEvery=1` gives 1.7729 against
      `=16`'s 1.7701); the refinement margin (that static leg ran
      `?margin=22`); the moving window (+43.1% with `?window=0`, and the same
      control on the dense path is +2.1% against its windowed -5.3%); D1's
      swept-cell term (+39% with `?swept=0`); the interface scheme (`interp`
      reads 1.9025 against explode's 1.7701); and tile granularity (RB = 8
      reads +43.3%). The depth ladder falsified the best structural
      hypothesis -- "one pose per macro-step, several substeps" predicts the
      error GROWS with depth and it does the opposite (+34.2% at depth 2 with
      two substeps per pose, +7.8% at depth 3 with four).

      **THE BIFURCATION THAT CRACKED IT: ENSTROPHY IS EXACTLY
      FRAME-INVARIANT.** Vorticity does not see a uniform velocity shift, so
      the pinned and towed legs must report the same enstrophy whatever their
      Cd says. They do -- 1.003x on the dense path AND 1.003x on the pool,
      while the reported Cd differs by 19% and 47%. **So the FIELDS AGREE and
      only the force READING differs.** That retires the fresh-node refill,
      which D1's first write-up named as the likely residual: a refill defect
      would change the flow, and the flow is not changed.

      **AND THEN THE MOMENTUM BUDGET AGAINST tau.** In a periodic sponge-free
      box the fluid's momentum can only change through the body, so
      `sum F = -dP` exactly, and the shortfall is the force the kernel is not
      measuring. At one U, one body and one domain:

          tau      0.509     0.55      0.6      0.8
          short    16.6%     3.6%     1.5%     0.0%
          x(tau-1/2)  1.5e-3  1.8e-3  1.5e-3    --

      **THE PRODUCT IS CONSTANT: the deficit goes as 1/(tau - 1/2)**, which at
      fixed U and D is linear in Reynolds number. And 16.6% at tau = 0.509 is
      the dense frame gap of 18.5%, arrived at by a completely different
      measurement.

      **THE PREDICTION, AND IT HELD ON BOTH PATHS.** If that is the mechanism,
      the Galilean split must go FLAT at tau = 0.6 everywhere. Measured, same
      body, same U:

                      pinned    a=0.5     a=1     frame gap
          dense       5.8957    5.8951   5.8481     -0.8%
          AMR L2      5.8418    5.8961   5.9013     +1.0%

      Flat on both, and the two paths agree with EACH OTHER to ~1%, against
      -18.5% and +47.3% at tau = 0.509. **So D3 is D1, and neither is a pool
      bug.** The opposite SIGNS at tau = 0.509 are two discretizations of one
      degrading coupling, not two defects.

      **WHY NOTHING CAUGHT IT.** A PINNED body at low tau is fine --
      sphere-Re200-D16 runs at tau = 0.512 and sits +7.0% against
      Schiller-Naumann -- because its wall-position error is a fixed offset
      that lands inside the standing staircase allowance. It is the MOVING
      coupling that degrades, and until D1 nothing in this suite moved a body
      and measured its drag.

      **THE RULE THAT FALLS OUT, and it is the useful half: NO MOVING-BODY
      DRAG NUMBER BELOW tau ~ 0.55 IS TRUSTWORTHY.** benchmarks/d3.json
      already records that tau = 0.9330127 is where Lambda = (tau-1/2)^2
      reaches 3/16 and halfway bounce-back places the wall EXACTLY halfway,
      and that the duct cases sit deliberately off it "to keep the
      wall-position error visible rather than tuned away". This is that same
      parameter biting a hundred times harder on a body that moves:
      tau = 0.509 gives Lambda = 8.1e-5, which is 2300x below the magic value.

      **AND IT SHARPENS M8.0's ARGUMENT RATHER THAN CONTRADICTING IT.** M8.0
      says the stability margin is bought with RESOLUTION, because
      tau = 1/2 + 6 u_t a / Re, and that AMR is the stability mechanism for
      the falling card rather than an optimization. Accuracy now says the same
      thing for the same reason: `tauAtLevel` doubles (tau - 1/2) per rung, so
      a body on a refined level is not merely more stable, its moving-body
      coupling is proportionally more accurate. The card's target at n = 64,
      Re = 1100 is tau = 0.5087 DENSE -- squarely in the bad regime -- and two
      rungs of refinement put its own level at tau = 0.535.

      **WHAT IS NOT ESTABLISHED.** Why the coupling degrades this way. The
      1/(tau - 1/2) law is four points at one U and one D, fitted after the
      fact; it should be checked at a second U and a second D before being
      leaned on quantitatively. The likely frame is Ginzburg & d'Humieres'
      analysis of bounce-back's Lambda-dependent wall location, which is
      well-developed for a STATIC wall and is what a moving one needs
      extending to -- a TRT or two-relaxation-time collision fixes Lambda
      independently of tau and is the standard remedy, and would be a real
      change to this solver rather than a tuning knob.

      **RULED OUT AND DERIVED, so it is not re-litigated: the wall density in
      Ladd's correction.** The momentum-exchange force is Galilean invariant
      to FIRST order in the frame velocity iff the correction carries the
      LOCAL density rather than a pinned 1 -- the two halves of the sum shift
      by `+SUM 2 w_i rho e_i(e_i.V)/cs^2` and `-SUM 2 w_i rho_w e_i(e_i.V)/cs^2`
      and cancel exactly when rho_w = rho. That is now what all four kernels
      do (`RHO_W_LOCAL`, `?rhow=0` to A/B, and identically zero on a pinned
      body because the correction itself vanishes at u_w = 0). **It is worth
      0.04%**: the split moves from -18.5% to -18.5%, Cd 1.1377 to 1.1373.
      Front (rho > 1) and rear (rho < 1) contributions cancel over a closed
      surface. The change is kept because it is the correct formula and the
      guarded loads cost nothing, NOT because it fixed anything -- and the
      measurement is here so the next person does not spend the afternoon on
      it that this one did.

- [ ] **D4 — WHICH COUPLING FOR A MOVING BODY? THE DIFFUSE ONE IS FRAME-
      CONSISTENT AND NOISELESS AND THE BODY IS THE WRONG SIZE.** Opened
      2026-09-11 out of D3, because D3's answer ("the moving-body bounce-back
      coupling degrades as tau -> 1/2") makes the coupling itself a live
      choice again rather than a settled one.

      **THE MEASUREMENT, at exactly the configuration where bounce-back is
      worst** -- D = 12, Re = 100, tau = 0.509, U = 0.025, the Galilean split
      (`tools/probe-d3-galilean.js --diffuse`):

          coupling        pinned    a=0.5      a=1     frame gap   rms/mean
          bounce-back     1.3951   1.2593   1.1373      -18.5%       295%
          diffuse (chi)   5.0433   5.1609   5.1419       +2.0%       0.5%

      **TWO THINGS AT ONCE, and they are both large.** The frame gap is 9x
      better and the force NOISE is 600x better: a moving bounce-back body's
      instantaneous force swings about three times its own drag as the
      staircase reshapes, and the diffuse one does not swing at all. That
      second column also retires the entire aliasing hazard
      tools/probe-d3-window.js's header documents, because there is nothing
      periodic to alias.

      **AND IT IS PREDICTED, not just observed.** Momentum exchange is a raw
      sum of INDIVIDUAL populations over a SUBSET of directions, which is not
      a moment, so non-hydrodynamic (ghost) content enters it directly. The
      diffuse penalty reads `rho` and `u*`, which ARE moments and which ghost
      modes are orthogonal to by construction. If D3's residual is ghost
      content pumped by a moving boundary and damped at a rate proportional to
      (2 - omega) = 2(tau - 1/2)/tau -- which is the 1/(tau-1/2) law D3
      measured -- then the diffuse coupling cannot have it. It does not.
      (Stated as a hypothesis the data is consistent with; what is MEASURED is
      the law, that it is moving-only, and that the two couplings differ by
      9x on the same flow.)

      **THE PRICE, AND IT IS NOT SMALL: THE BODY IS THE WRONG SIZE.** Diffuse
      pinned Cd is 5.0433 against Schiller-Naumann's 1.0917 -- **+362%**. Back
      an effective radius out of it (Cd ~ R^2) and the D = 12 sphere is
      behaving like D ~ 26. That is the same open diffuse-interface-width
      issue benchmarks/d3.json's sphere_tolerances_note already records at
      +49..131% for D = 16/24; it is worse here because D = 12 is coarser and
      because in 3D the frontal-area error goes as (1 + d/R)^2.

      **SO IT IS A REGULARIZATION, NOT A WRONG MODEL.** Brinkman penalization
      converges to no-slip as the band width and the permeability go to zero;
      at finite width it is honestly solving a slightly different problem, a
      porous body with a fuzzy edge, with a controlled error. "Non-physical"
      is the wrong word. "Convergent, and not converged at these widths" is
      the right one.

      **WHICH IS WHY AMR REOPENS THE QUESTION.** `CHI_SCALE = 2^-m` and `phi`
      is in L0 units at every level, so the band is CHI_EPS cells AT THE LEVEL
      THAT RESOLVES THE BODY and its PHYSICAL width halves per rung: 1.5
      coarse units dense, 0.75 at depth 2, 0.375 at depth 3. Refinement
      therefore attacks the diffuse coupling's only real weakness
      automatically, at the same time as it attacks bounce-back's by doubling
      (tau - 1/2) per rung. **The 2D choice of chi was right for its era** (a
      phone, no AMR, resolution-starved, so a fat body was the lesser evil)
      **and the 3D choice of bounce-back was right for its era** (sharp,
      second-order, exact geometry). Neither argument survives AMR unchanged.

      **MEASURED: IT CONVERGES, AT SECOND ORDER IN THE BAND WIDTH, TO THE
      NUMBER BOUNCE-BACK GIVES DIRECTLY.** Two ladders, because refining
      shrinks the band AND resolves the flow and those had to be separated.
      D = 12, Re = 100 (Schiller-Naumann 1.0917), tau = 0.509, pinned:

          band (coarse cells)   dense ?chiEps=     refined ?levels=
          1.500                 5.080  (+365%)     5.080  (L1)
          0.750                 2.413  (+121%)     2.927  (L2, chiEps=1.5)
          0.375                 1.721   (+58%)     2.017  (L3, chiEps=1.5)

      **THE BAND LADDER IS SECOND ORDER**: the Cd differences are -2.667 and
      -0.692, a ratio of 3.85 against 4 for halving. A Richardson
      extrapolation to zero band gives **Cd = 1.478, i.e. +35%** -- against
      BOUNCE-BACK's 1.3951, +28%, on the same body. The two couplings converge
      to the same discrete-body drag, which is the check that says the diffuse
      path is a regularization of the same problem and not a different one.
      So chi at best MATCHES bounce-back's accuracy; its advantage is entirely
      in the moving case.

      **AND THE FRAME CONSISTENCY SURVIVES SHARPENING, which was the thing
      most at risk** -- a narrower band is a stiffer force. Measured tow
      against pinned: **-0.2% at L3 with chiEps = 1.5**, and -1.9% on the
      dense sub-cell band (chiEps = 0.375). Against bounce-back's -18.5% at
      the same tau. Force RMS stays at 2% of the mean or better in every
      diffuse leg, against 295%.

      **AT A FIXED PHYSICAL BAND, REFINING IS WORSE, and the reason is the
      interesting part.** 2.017 (L3, chiEps 1.5) against 1.721 (dense, chiEps
      0.375) at the same 0.375 coarse cells. The dense leg's band is
      SUB-CELL, so `tanh(phi/eps)` is nearly a step and the body is nearly
      sharp -- it is quietly approximating bounce-back, which is why its Cd is
      lower. It keeps most of chi's frame consistency (-1.9%) but it is no
      longer the smooth interface the argument for chi rests on, and there is
      nothing left to shrink.

      **SO THE COMBINATION THAT WOULD SHIP IS BOTH KNOBS AT ONCE:** refine so
      the band is several cells wide AT THE BODY'S OWN LEVEL (smooth, hence
      frame-consistent and quiet) while its PHYSICAL width is small (hence a
      body near the right size). At depth 3 a `?chiEps=` of about 0.8 is a
      band of 3.2 cells on the body's level and 0.2 coarse cells physically,
      which the ladder above puts near the +35% floor. That is the next
      measurement, and it is one run.

      **A REPORTING BUG THIS TURNED UP, now fixed.** `fall`'s `cdReference`
      was `schillerNaumann(re)`, and `re` is the Reynolds number of the TARGET
      terminal velocity `u_t` -- correct for a free fall, where it also drives
      g_eff, and wrong for a prescribed leg running at `uRel`. With
      uRel = 0.025 against u_t = 0.04 the logs compared against Re = 160 while
      the flow was at Re = 100, which read as +474% where the honest figure
      was +365%. No gate reads it (the prescribed cases score against each
      other or against recorded values), but a log that lies is how a number
      gets quoted later.

      **AND ON A PLATE, WHICH IS THE GEOMETRY THAT MATTERS HERE.** The sphere
      ladder understates the risk: the band adds an ABSOLUTE thickness to a
      body whose defining property is being thin, and a chord-16 plate is TWO
      cells thick against a default band of 1.5. Measured, pinned, Re = 200,
      broadside, against the square-plate 1.18 and the bounce-back gate:

          band     chord 16 (2c thick)    chord 24 (3c thick)
          1.500      2.9905  (+153%)        2.1927   (+86%)
          0.750      1.8382   (+56%)        1.5638   (+33%)
          0.375      1.4946   (+27%)        1.3601   (+15%)
          bounce-back 1.2066  (+2.3%)       1.2012   (+1.8%)

      Better than feared and worse than the sphere: convergence in the band is
      between first and second order (difference ratios 3.35 and 3.08 for
      halving), extrapolating to ~1.35 at chord 16 and ~1.26 at chord 24
      against bounce-back's 1.20. So the plate converges too, and it converges
      toward the same place, but it needs a NARROWER band than a sphere does to
      get there -- which is the thin-body penalty stated as a number.

      **THE FRAME GAP AND THE NOISE HOLD UP ON THE PLATE, AND THE STABILITY IS
      THE REAL RESULT.** Towed at chord 24, band 0.375: frame gap **-2.1%**,
      force RMS 3.5% of the mean. The bounce-back plate's half-tow leg carries
      an RMS of **931%** of its mean, and its FULL tow at chord 16 went
      NON-FINITE (benchmarks/d3.json's plate_note records that refusal). **So
      on a moving plate chi is not merely more accurate in frame terms, it runs
      where bounce-back does not.**

      **AND ON THE CARD ITSELF, which is what all of this is for.** A/B at
      n = 32, tilt = 0.15, dense, 16000 steps, chi at the DEFAULT band
      (chiEps = 1.5, so 1.5 coarse cells against a plate 4 cells thick):

                          v_x      v_x/u_t   |f|/mg    rho span    max|u|
          Re=200  b-back  0.0420    0.840    1.002    +-1.7%      0.069
          Re=200  chi     0.0327    0.654    1.000    +-0.3%      0.069->0.054
          Re=500  b-back  0.0420    0.840    1.001    +-1.7%      0.071
          Re=500  chi     0.0316    0.632    0.999    +-0.3%      0.052

      **BOTH CLOSE THE FREE-FALL BALANCE TO 1.000**, both reach a terminal
      velocity, both fall broadside without tumbling (|omega| ~ 1e-4), and all
      four survived. So chi CARRIES THE CARD -- that is the headline, and it
      was not obvious, because a 1.5-cell band on a 4-cell plate is a large
      perturbation to the body's defining dimension.

      **THE 22% SLOWER FALL IS THE FAT BODY, QUANTITATIVELY.** Terminal
      velocity goes as 1/sqrt(Cd), and the pinned-plate ladder above puts chi
      at chiEps = 1.5 near +60% of Cd at this chord, predicting
      v_chi/v_bb = 1/sqrt(1.6) = 0.79. Measured 0.78. That agreement is what
      says the difference is the known, convergent band error and not a second
      defect -- the same body seen through a thicker edge.

      **AND chi's DENSITY FIELD IS FIVE TIMES QUIETER**: rho within +-0.3%
      against bounce-back's +-1.7%. A moving staircase radiates pressure waves
      every time a cell flips, and rho excursions are what precede a blowup, so
      this is a stability-relevant margin and not a cosmetic one.

      **AND NARROWING THE BAND WALKS THE CARD ONTO BOUNCE-BACK'S ANSWER.**
      Re = 500, n = 32, 26000 steps, terminal `v_x`:

          band 1.500   0.0316    0.632 x bounce-back
          band 0.750   0.0358    0.842
          band 0.375   0.0428    1.007
          bounce-back  0.0425    1.000

      **0.7% apart at band 0.375**, with the free-fall balance at 1.00 the
      whole way. That is the D4 ladder's prediction met on the real case: the
      diffuse error is the band, the band is a knob, and turning it down
      recovers the sharp-body answer. It is also the practical form of the AMR
      argument -- 0.375 coarse cells is what `?levels=3` gives for free at the
      DEFAULT `chiEps`, because the band is a fixed number of cells at the
      body's own level.

      **AND THE Re = 500 STABILITY WALL IS UNMOVED BY THE COUPLING, WHICH IS
      THE TAXONOMY BEARING OUT.** This file records the Re = 500 bounce-back
      card diverging at ~20000 steps. Ran four ways to 26000-30000:

          default (swept + rhow on)      26000 clean
          ?swept=0&rhow=0  (pre-D1)      DIED at ~25000
          ?swept=0  only                 30000 clean
          chi, band 0.375                26000 clean

      **So the recorded divergence reproduces in SHAPE and not in STEP**, and
      the step is not a usable discriminator: a pre-D1 configuration died at
      ~25000 while another disabled-feature combination ran 30000, which is
      exactly the "single draws good to about a factor of two" D2 already
      warns about for this case. **Do NOT read this as "D1's fix extended the
      card's life"** -- the evidence does not support it, and the one
      configuration that died differs from a survivor by a change measured at
      0.04% of the force.

      **What it DOES establish is the more useful thing: chi does not move the
      wall either.** That is predicted rather than disappointing. The Re = 500
      blowup is the FIRST tau limit -- ghost modes undamped as omega -> 2 --
      which is a property of the BULK collision operator, and no boundary
      treatment touches it (plans/TRT.md sec 6). chi buys the moving-boundary
      limit and the noise; it does not buy the NaNs, and now that is measured
      rather than argued.

      **AN OBSERVATION WORTH FOLLOWING, NOT A RESULT: the chi card may be
      starting to TURN.** On the band-0.375 leg |omega| grows monotonically
      through the last third -- 1.7e-4, 2.0e-4, 2.7e-4, 3.1e-4, 5.6e-4, 8.8e-4
      at steps 16000..26000 -- while `v_x` wanders 0.0398 to 0.0460 and the
      bounce-back leg's |omega| stays flat at ~5e-5. That is either the onset
      of the flutter this whole milestone exists to produce, or a slow
      numerical drift, and 8000 steps does not separate them. **One speculative
      reason it would be chi and not bounce-back**: the bounce-back plate's
      force RMS is ~900% of its mean and its rho swings +-1.7%, which is a
      large stochastic forcing on a body whose instability is a slow
      bifurcation -- a quiet field may simply let a real mode grow where a
      noisy one swamps it. Worth a long run before anyone believes it.

      **AND THE REAL FIX FOR THE tau FAMILY IS STILL TRT** -- see plans/TRT.md,
      which is the design note for it: pin `omega_-` so Lambda = 3/16 and damp
      the odd ghosts at O(1) while `omega_+` still carries the viscosity. That
      addresses the NaNs AND the wall position AND the moving-body reading at
      once, and KEEPS bounce-back's exact geometry, where chi trades the
      coupling defect for a sizing one. The two are not exclusive; TRT is the
      one that also buys the instability, which is what currently stops the
      card at Re = 500.

- [ ] **D2 — THE CARD AT Re = 500 DIVERGES WHEN ITS ROTATION IS FREE**, at
      ~9000 steps under AMR against ~23000 dense. Unexplained; none of M8.7,
      M8.8 or M8.9 addressed it.

      **THE DISCRIMINATOR IS THE ROTATION.** `?omax=0` -- the same
      configuration with the plate held from turning -- runs past step 22750
      healthy (`v_x` = 0.042, max|u| = 0.114), where the free-rotation leg is
      already gone (`v_x` = -0.060, max|u| = 0.402).

          rotation free, diverges at ~9000 (about 30 s):
          index-3d.html?scenario=card&n=32&re=500&u_t=0.05&aspect=0.125&span=1
            &tilt=0.15&i_star=0.17&q=19&bounceback=1&axis=z&mode=vorticity
            &spf=10&levels=2&rb=4&refine=body&interface=explode&dynamic=1
            &manageEvery=16&margin=3&slots=3000

          rotation frozen, the control:   ...&omax=0

      **RULED OUT: geometry coverage.** It DOES fail transiently for the plate
      (2 cells of 13796 at step 4000, 3 at 5500) and step 4000 is exactly where
      max|u| first jumps with the hot cell at level 1 and `sdf` ~ 0 -- on the
      plate's own surface -- after which it never returns to baseline. That
      reads as causal and is not: `?manageMargin=8` widens the MANAGER's reach
      while leaving the CHECKER's requirement at `?margin=3`, removing every
      violation, and the run still dies at the same step with coverage green at
      every sample including the one before the blowup.

      **ALSO RULED OUT: the rotation LEAD** (M8.8). That leg's coverage was
      already clean, so the lead was not what was missing.

      **TWO CAVEATS ON THE NUMBERS.** The card at Re = 500 is marginal in every
      configuration and AMR is not bit-reproducible (the atomicSub free list),
      so the divergence STEPS are single draws good to about a factor of two --
      the qualitative split is solid, the number is not. And `?omax=0` does not
      only freeze rotation, it zeroes angular momentum every step, so it is a
      blunt control rather than a clean one. A cleaner one would cap `|omega|`
      at a physical value rather than at zero.

- [ ] **M8.5 — THE EPHEMERAL TRAVERSAL STRUCTURE, and why it is not an
      octree.** Sketched 2026-09-10. Placed after M8.4 because M8.4 is what
      creates the need: geometry-forced refinement gives ONE compact region,
      which M6.4's bounding boxes already handle, and a wake-following
      criterion does not.

      **THE BRICK IS ALREADY THE TILE, and that is the whole shortcut.**
      RB = 4 makes a tile interior exactly 8^3 cells, which is the classic
      macrocell brick size. So the acceleration brick IS a pool slot: no
      scatter, no atomics, no allocation -- one workgroup per tile reducing
      cells it already owns. Measured against the flagship's real pool:

          L0 dense 192x128x128, 8^3 blocks    6144 bricks    72 KB
          L1 pool  (measured)                  432 bricks     5 KB
          L2 pool  (measured)                 1280 bricks    15 KB
                                                             -----
                                                             92 KB

      against **2.25 GB** for the dense finest volume M6.1 refuses to build.
      The rebuild reads 4.0M cells once, ~0.11 ms. The entire structure fits
      in L2 cache, and "discarded atomically each frame" becomes
      "overwritten in place" -- there is nothing to free.

      **SO THE ARENA IS NOT NEEDED, AND THAT IS A MEASUREMENT RATHER THAN A
      preference.** A bump allocator with one atomicAdd and a per-frame
      reset is the right design for a SPARSE tree; it earns nothing while
      the dense structure is 92 KB. Even a full min/max pyramid over the
      FINEST index space (768x512x512 at 8^3 bricks, plus three 8:1 levels)
      is 3.43 MB. **Dense stops being free at about 4096^3 finest**, where
      the macrocell grid alone reaches 1 GB; that is the crossover, and it
      is two doublings past anything this project will run.

      **TWO JOBS, SEPARATED -- which is what kills the 2.25 GB.**
      - *Where is there anything worth sampling* -> this structure. It
        carries `(min, max, finestLevelWithin)` and NEVER field data.
      - *What is the value here* -> `sampleTree()` (M6.0), already gated
        against the host by `debugCheckTreeSample`.
      Conflating them is what makes people build a dense volume.

      Sketch:

          per frame
            A  one workgroup per tile  -> (min,max) of Q over its 8^3 interior
            B  one workgroup per 8^3 L0 block -> (min,max) + finestLevelWithin
               (the latter is blockSlot lookups the sampler already does)
            C  optional 8:1 pyramid over B: 72 KB -> 9 KB -> 1 KB, free

          per ray  -- a hierarchical DDA
            3D-DDA over L0 bricks (Amanatides-Woo)
              transfer-function support ∩ [min,max] empty
                  -> advance a WHOLE brick, zero samples
              otherwise
                  -> step at 2^-finestLevelWithin, calling sampleTree();
                     the sampler already returns the local `h`, so the
                     adaptive sampling RATE falls out instead of being a
                     second rule that can disagree with the first.

      **WHAT SURVIVES OF THE ARENA IDEA, and it is the valuable half:
      TRAVERSAL-ORDER-AWARE LAYOUT.** In the dense scheme that is the INDEX
      FUNCTION, and dense gives freer choice than sparse does -- Morton-order
      the L0 bricks so consecutive samples along any ray direction stay near
      in memory. In a sparse scheme it is the allocation ORDER, and the trick
      is to allocate children in the order a ray would visit them.

      **LITERATURE, mapped to the parts rather than listed.**
      - **ExaBricks** (Wald, Zellmann, Usher, Morrical, Lang, Pascucci,
        VIS 2020, arXiv:2009.03076) is THIS problem -- ray tracing structured
        AMR. Its ACTIVE BRICK REGIONS are a precomputed partition where each
        region knows which bricks are active in it, so a ray learns which
        segments to integrate AND AT WHAT FREQUENCY from one lookup. That is
        the adaptive-rate problem above, solved properly, and it is the one
        piece to take outright. Follow-up: Zellmann et al., "Beyond
        ExaBricks: GPU Volume Path Tracing of AMR Data", CGF 2024.
      - **NanoVDB** (Museth, SIGGRAPH 2021) is the closest existing thing to
        an "ephemeral arena octree": fixed shallow depth, a BITMASK per level
        instead of per-child pointers (child indexing is a popcount), a
        linear layout that is memcpy-able with no CPU address dependence, and
        hierarchical DDAs shipped specifically for empty-space skipping. If
        sparse ever becomes necessary, copy this layout rather than inventing
        one.
      - **Revelles, Ureña & Lastra 2000** ("An Efficient Parametric Algorithm
        for Octree Traversal", WSCG) is "traversal order in mind" literally:
        top-down, child selection driven by the ray parameter and reduced to
        simple comparisons, children visited in ray order.
      - Foundations all of the above build on: Amanatides & Woo's 3D-DDA
        (1987), Levoy's hierarchical empty-space skipping (1990), Krüger &
        Westermann's GPU early-ray-termination and space-skipping (2003).

      **MEASURE THE OCCUPANCY FIRST, and be willing to not build this.**
      Empty-space skipping pays in proportion to how much space is empty. A
      histogram of Q over the pool gives the interesting fraction directly
      and costs one reduction over data already in memory. If it comes back
      near 50%, the whole structure buys ~2x and the effort belongs
      elsewhere. Do that measurement early -- it is nearly free, it is the
      same shape as `tools/probe-d3-tau.js`, and M8.0 is the standing
      reminder of what happens when a number like this is assumed instead.

**M7 — `?q=27` variant.** Cheap once the generated-table split from M1 is
in place. (Deliberately *not* paired with an f16 re-measurement -- see
§2.2. If f16 is ever revisited it is on the strength of a new format, not
a new dimension.)

**Out of scope for v1:** isosurface extraction, LES, multi-GPU, TRT/MRT.

(The moving window was on this list and came OFF it in M8.3, 2026-09-11 --
not because the scope grew but because M8.2b showed no moving-body
measurement can converge without one. It also turned out not to be the
`off_x`-style domain translation this line assumed: see M8.3.)

### Rough size

WGSL is 5292 lines today across 31 files. A 3D set with both velocity sets
handled by generated tables: **~7000–8500 lines**, of which perhaps 40% is
mechanical translation and the rest is genuinely new (manage/balance in 3D,
Q-criterion, 6-DOF, volume render). JS: `main-amr.js` is 3819 lines; a
`main-3d-amr.js` should land **~4000–5000**, plus a `main-3d.js` at ~700.
Plus 3D siblings for `tools/lib/` metrics, the invariant checker, and the
`validate-all.js` config table.

**This is comparable in size to the entire AMR-multilevel arc (M1–M10)**,
not to a single milestone from it.

---

## 6. Fork or parameterize?

CLAUDE.md records the 238e48c failure mode: a WGSL binding-count change
mirrored into `main-cylinder-amr.js`'s bind group but not `main-amr.js`'s
separate copy of the same one, which broke a production page and failed
nothing, because no test had ever loaded it. A 3D fork doubles that
surface.

Parameterizing one shader set over `DIM` is nonetheless worse. WGSL has no
preprocessor beyond this repo's own `@include`, no templates, and array
sizes must be constant — so `array<f32, Q>` with Q varying by dimension
forces override-baked pipeline variants of every kernel anyway, and the
index arithmetic, neighbour enumeration, bilinear-vs-trilinear and
quadrant-vs-octant decode all differ structurally, not by a constant.

**Recommended middle path:**

1. **Fork the shader files** (`shaders/d3_*.wgsl`), so a 2D change cannot
   break 3D by accident and vice versa.
2. **Share the genuinely dimension-agnostic includes** where they really
   are agnostic (`common_reduce` after generalizing to 6 outputs;
   `common_fpack`'s lo/hi/word helpers after generalizing the plane count).
3. **Generate the lattice tables from a shared `.mjs`**, asserted by a
   unit test, exactly as `f-pack.mjs` + `tools/test-f-pack.js` already do.
   A transposed `opp` table is the highest-consequence, lowest-visibility
   bug available in this port.
4. **Add every 3D page to `runBootSmoke` in `tools/validate-all.js` on the
   day it is created**, not after it breaks. That check exists precisely
   because a page nothing visited stayed broken in production.

---

## 7. Ranked risks

1. ~~**Q=27 register spilling** (§2.4).~~ **RETIRED 2026-09-09 by M0.**
   Measured: the full kernel matches its own stream-only bandwidth ceiling
   at both velocity sets, at 77%/71% of device peak. No spill. Nothing
   downstream needs the restructuring contingency, and §2.4's numbers are
   the new anchor for every throughput figure in §3.
2. **3D 2:1 balance in `manage_pool`.** The 2D version's own header
   documents three live-verified bugs found only by
   `debugCheck21Balance`; 3D has 6 face-neighbours, 8 children, and 26
   ghost neighbours. Mitigation: port the invariant checker *before* the
   manager, and assert periodically through a run as the 2D tool already
   does — a transient violation must not slip past.
3. **Refinement threshold retuning.** The 2D thresholds will not transfer,
   and §3.3 shows a 2x wall-clock swing between sparse and generous
   refinement. This is a tuning campaign, not a constant.
4. **Memory / binding limits** (§2.2). Well-understood, and the
   raised-limit machinery exists — but it caps what resolutions are
   reachable and should be decided before the pool layout is fixed, not
   after.
5. **Coupling overhead may be worse than modelled — now risk #1 by
   default.** With M0 done this is the ONLY remaining assumption in every
   throughput figure in §3 (the solve rate is measured; the coupling
   fraction is not). 2D measures 40–49% of frame; the surface-to-volume
   argument says 3D is worse, and §3.2 assumes only "somewhat". If it lands
   at 65%, every throughput number above drops ~30%. Measurable at M3 with
   the existing `?benchSkip=` groups, which is the earliest point it can be
   known — and it should be the first thing M3 measures, not the last.
6. **Volume rendering eats the schedule.** Mitigated as planned: M1 shipped
   the slice view (`shaders/d3_render_slice.wgsl`) and the raymarcher stays
   at M6. One lesson worth carrying there: `common_vortcolor.wgsl`'s tone
   curve is calibrated for a 2D shedding wake at |omega| ~ 0.05, and every
   3D field so far is an order of magnitude gentler, so the page normalizes
   by a scenario-derived reference and pins VORT_SCALE to 1. A transfer
   function that is not normalized to the actual field renders a correct
   simulation as a black screen.

## 8. Next three things to do

**Rewritten 2026-09-11.** The previous list was stale in a way worth naming,
because this file is the first place anyone looks for what is open: all three
of its items had closed and it still read as the plan. ~~M0, the register
spike~~ (done, §2.4), ~~ghost-free vs. materialized~~ (decided, §2.1 — the
RING, final), ~~M1~~, ~~M2~~, ~~M3: the octree pool~~ (done, and M4/M5 took it
to any depth), and ~~choose RB~~ (RB=4 ships; §2.1's storage table is the
reasoning).

1. ~~**A TRT (two-relaxation-time) collision, or an equivalent fix for Lambda.**~~
   **DONE AND ANSWERED IN THE NEGATIVE, 2026-09-13 -- plans/TRT.md sec 8.10-8.14;
   code on branch `worktree-3d-trt`, not merged.** Built, gated, measured:
   it fixes the pinned straight wall and DOUBLES the moving-body deficit
   while LOWERING the stability wall at this project's Mach. Lambda was not
   the mechanism of D3. The item's lasting result is that the f32 lattice
   weights did not sum to 1. The original text follows, kept as the
   prediction it was:
   D1 and D3 closed together on one finding: the moving-body bounce-back
   coupling degrades as tau -> 1/2, with the force short as 1/(tau - 1/2), and
   at tau = 0.6 the Galilean split is flat on both the dense path and the pool.
   Lambda = (tau-1/2)^2 is 8.1e-5 at the card's own target tau of 0.5087
   against the 3/16 at which halfway bounce-back is exact. TRT fixes Lambda
   independently of tau and is the standard remedy. First because the target
   lives exactly where this bites, because AMR only buys a factor of 2 per rung
   against it, and because it is the one change that would let the card be run
   at its own Reynolds number rather than at a tau the coupling can tolerate.
   Check the 1/(tau-1/2) law at a second U and a second D before leaning on it
   quantitatively -- it is four points at one of each, fitted after the fact.

2. **D2 — the card at Re = 500 with its rotation free.** Diverges at ~9000
   steps; `?omax=0` runs past 22750. Same section. Second because the
   discriminator is known (rotation) but the mechanism is not. **The line that
   used to stand here -- "D1 may turn out to be its cause" -- is now
   answerable and should be answered before anything else is tried: D1's fix
   is one URL parameter away (`?swept=0`), so re-run that leg both ways and
   see whether the divergence step moves at all.** It is an AMR leg, so D3
   sits under it too.
3. **Read `AGAL/src/solver_lbm/` with `VSET=D3Q19` in mind.** Carried
   forward unchanged from the previous list, and still the cheapest way to
   find out whether any of the above is a solved problem elsewhere in this
   repo. It was listed for M3/M4 and never read; the octree landed without
   it, but the moving-body coupling is exactly the kind of thing a reference
   implementation would have already gotten right.

**And one process item, from a day that produced three correctness fixes in
one criterion and two retractions.** Every body in this suite was a smooth
SPHERE until M8.6, and a smooth sphere exercises none of the three bugs the
card found inside an hour (a sphere-shaped `sdfAt`, a saturating violation
count, a non-conservative block test). **Adding a shape the suite has never
seen is a higher-yield move than any individual fix**, and the same is
probably true of the other axes: no case has a body that both translates and
rotates under fluid torque, and none runs above Re = 500.
